#5113. North Korean hackers launder $242 million of Binance loot via Tron as FBI presses THORChain: analyst

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Relay and THORChain moved the largest shares of the loot off Tron, while Symbiosis and Chainflip said their screening had flaws. THORChain’s official front end has blocked the FBI-listed addresses.

North Korean hackers have laundered about $242 million of the funds stolen from Binance on the Tron blockchain in two days, onchain analyst and Security Alliance (SEAL) contributor SomaXBT said on X on Sunday, as the FBI presses THORChain and other services to block the transactions.

The attackers took about $628 million from Binance on Tron and had roughly $249 million left to move after Tether froze 378.7 million USDT, SomaXBT said in his thread. Most of the $242 million, about $194 million, left Tron through bridges and swap services. Another $26 million stayed on the network, while about $22 million went to fees, slippage and price moves, according to the analyst.

The funds moved through a variety of crypto bridges: Relay handled $48 million of the outflows, THORChain $44 million, Chainflip $32.1 million and Symbiosis $16 million, together about $140 million, SomaXBT said. Bitget Wallet handled another $41.7 million, but where that money ended up is still unresolved, the analyst said.

Binance lost about $6.3 billion on Thursday in the largest crypto hack on record. The FBI attributed the theft to the TraderTraitor group on Saturday in an alert issued with the U.S. Secret Service and IRS Criminal Investigation.

The FBI said the hackers were converting stolen assets "primarily into Ethereum" and expects the funds to be further laundered through DeFi protocols and instant swap services. It urged THORChain's THORNode operators, alongside RPC node operators, instant exchanges, DeFi services and other providers, to block transactions tied to the theft. The agency also published a list of 608 Ethereum addresses, 19 bitcoin addresses and one Solana address, but no Tron addresses, that it said were linked to the attack.

A 2022 joint advisory from the FBI, the Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency and the Treasury Department describes TraderTraitor as a North Korean state-sponsored hacking group, active since at least 2020, that steals and launders crypto "to support the North Korean regime." The advisory says the cybersecurity industry tracks the group under various designations, including Lazarus Group, APT38 and BlueNoroff.

The FBI also blamed TraderTraitor for the $1.5 billion Bybit hack in February 2025. The agency's recent alert did not disclose the evidence that led it to its attribution, though blockchain analysis firms Chainalysis, Elliptic and TRM Labs had already linked the theft to North Korea through onchain ties to earlier North Korean hacks.

THORChain technical co-founder Chad Barraford wrote on X on Saturday evening that "THORNode operators have zero information that isn't already public and readily available in real time." He added, "No subpoenas required, a best case scenario for investigations," but his posts since the hack have not said whether node operators would block the listed addresses.

Earlier on Saturday, an Ethereum reorg caused THORChain to double-refund a 10 ETH deposit, which triggered an emergency pause, Chainalysis said. The pause, somewhat ironically, stopped all users, including the hackers, from swapping through most of THORChain's bridges, according to the firm.

THORChain trading remained halted on 10 of its 13 connected chains, including Tron, Ethereum and Bitcoin, as of 1:20 p.m. ET Sunday, according to THORChain node data. Trading from Tron has been halted since around midday Saturday.

In a message to The Block on Sunday, Barraford said he does not talk to node operators much and had not seen any discussion of the FBI’s request. "I assume they will be consistent and resume trading as normal," he said, adding that "the official thorchain centralized frontend has blocked the addresses requested by the FBI."

The attackers have sent 185.1 million TRX and 3.83 million USDT into THORChain in 2,697 deposits, SomaXBT said. THORChain has paid out 17,019 ETH to 170 addresses and 53.25 BTC to 20 addresses.

The attackers lost about 12% to fees and slippage, and front-end interfaces earned about 145,800 RUNE, roughly $95,000, in affiliate fees. North Korea's Lazarus Group also used THORChain to move much of the ether stolen from Bybit's $1.5 billion hack onto bitcoin in February 2025.

Relay received 37.64 million USDT and 39.28 million TRX in 735 deposits, SomaXBT said, adding that the protocol "barely featured" in laundering from past TraderTraitor hacks. Onchain analyst Specter said on Saturday that the hackers bridged $38 million through Relay in seven hours, despite Relay's statement that it screens transaction addresses using third-party risk data, including from Chainalysis, and an internal blocklist.

One Ethereum address received 2,804 ETH and 1.02 million USDT from Relay's router and solvers in 97 payments over about 80 minutes on Saturday, Etherscan data shows, matching SomaXBT's figures. The wallet swapped the USDT for ether on CoW Protocol and sent about 3,257 ETH, roughly $7.4 million, to four new addresses. None of the five addresses is on the FBI's list.

On Sunday, the hackers separately began swapping 970,522 SOL, worth about $95.9 million, for bitcoin through Relay, Lookonchain reported. The hackers' Solana wallet, the only Solana address on the FBI's list, moved 486,970 SOL ($46.4 million) in the hour before Lookonchain's 5:35 a.m. ET post. The Relay-powered swaps began after the FBI listed the wallet on Saturday. The wallet held about 466,700 SOL at publication time, the same balance as at 9:55 a.m. ET, per Solscan data.

Chainflip received 49.47 million USDT in 571 deposits, but many swaps failed, SomaXBT said. Failed swaps were refunded to fresh Tron wallets and sent back in, 17.91 million USDT in all. Chainflip planned an upgrade last year to block the Bybit hackers from its cross-chain DEX.

Chainflip said in a statement to The Block that it screens every deposit in real time against several anti-money laundering and blockchain analytics providers, whose lists include the addresses in the FBI alert, and rejects funds based on where they came from. Rejected deposits were refunded to fresh addresses that its providers did not flag as linked to the hack, so "when previously rejected funds were pushed back in from those addresses, some of them passed screening," said Shaun Janse van Vuuren, Chainflip’s head of growth and strategy.

Chainflip said it is working with its providers to tag refund addresses as linked to the original source immediately, a change it said has closed the workaround.

Symbiosis said early Sunday it had identified attempts to move Binance hack funds through its protocol and was in contact with Binance. "Our screening is built for normal flows, not for tracking fast-moving, sophisticated attempts so some mechanisms have gaps," Symbiosis said on X. "We're closing them as we go."

The attackers sent 49.71 million TRX into Symbiosis in 1,367 smaller deposits, SomaXBT said. Symbiosis recovered 15 BTC after an exploit of its own bitcoin bridge in September.

The hackers' main Tron distribution wallet paid out every hour for 31 straight hours and never paused for longer than 46 minutes, blockchain analytics firm Elliptic said on Saturday. The same swap amounts kept recurring in THORChain deposits, one of them 19 times.

When a bridge rejected deposits, new wallets automatically re-sent the funds to the same destination, Elliptic said. In the Bybit case, laundering followed human working patterns, according to the firm.

Elliptic co-founder Tom Robinson said in comments shared with The Block that the activity does not appear to involve AI agents. "The pattern is repetitive and static: the same sizes, the same rhythm, the same routes, hour after hour," Robinson said. He added that test transactions and round-number top-ups point to "operators making decisions, with scripts doing the repetitive work."

Elliptic has verified $2.85 billion of the stolen funds as frozen or seized, about 44% of the $6.43 billion it counts as stolen, and 94% of that is stablecoins. No funds have been frozen on Bitcoin, the XRP Ledger, Solana or NEAR. About $3.25 billion, including 18,327 BTC and 233 million XRP, is held in assets that no issuer can freeze, the firm said.

Tether's frozen USDT is split across two attacker wallets, with 312.7 million in one and 65.99 million in the other, per Tronscan data. Mempool.space data checked by The Block shows all 18,270 BTC still sitting in the 19 bitcoin addresses on the FBI's list.

Chainalysis said on Saturday the hackers have been depositing stablecoins into DeFi protocols on Tron for depository receipt tokens, which cannot be frozen, and later redeeming them from new addresses. It did not name the protocol, which other onchain analysts identified as JustLend, and did not address a question from The Block about whether it was JustLend.

TRM Labs said 300 million USDT moved to a Tron wallet that "began supplying it to the JustLend lending protocol." Tronscan data shows that wallet put 10,000 USDT into JustLend, swapped about 234 million USDT for TRX on decentralized exchanges and sent 65.99 million USDT to one of the two frozen wallets.

The Block has reached out to Relay and Bitget Wallet for comment.

Updated on Nov. 2 at 10:40 a.m. ET with statements from Chainflip and Chad Barraford.

#5112. 'My Workouts Stopped Working in Midlife. These 8 Lessons Helped Me Get Back on Track'

Coach Michaela Bentley shares these hard-earned lessons transformed her approach to strength, nutrition, and healthy aging.

At 60, Michaela Bentley knows exactly how to train, eat and recover. But that wasn't always the case.

“As someone who's always maintained a pretty high level of fitness, that all came to a crashing halt in my late 40s,” the personal trainer and nutrition coach, who specializes in women's midlife fitness, wrote on Instagram.

“I started packing on the pounds, and all the things I had done my entire life to stay fit stopped working,” she recalled. “I was putting on weight and couldn't figure out why since I was doing the same things I'd always done.”

Here, Bentley shares what she learned from navigating those changes, while Sarah Campus, personal trainer, instructor, nutrition coach, and founder of LDN MUMS FITNESS, adds her expert perspective.

Effective training can be pretty boring—and that's okay. You don't need a completely different workout every time you train.

Repeating key movements allows you to practice technique, build strength, and track progression, both experts say.

“If you're doing completely different workouts every week, you're not building strength,” says Bentley.

“Constantly changing everything makes it much harder to see whether you're actually getting stronger,” agrees Campus.

There is no magic number of reps that suddenly makes a workout effective, whether that's eight, 10, or 12.

“What matters more is that you are challenging your muscles and working with enough effort to create a stimulus,” says Campus. Your final few reps should feel difficult and bring you close to failure while still allowing you to maintain good technique.

Make sure your training also becomes more challenging over time, Bentley adds, by gradually increasing the weight, reps or training volume—otherwise known as progressive overload.

Doing random workouts can keep you active, but they'll usually deliver random results.

“A structured program gives your training purpose and allows for progressive overload,” Campus says.

In midlife, consistency and progression are far more valuable than constantly chasing the latest fitness trend, which may even increase your risk of injury, she adds.

Bentley recommends sticking with a program for at least six months to a year.

Particularly if fat loss is your goal, increasing your Non-Exercise Activity Thermogenesis—or NEAT—can help reduce body fat.

NEAT includes everyday movement such as housework, walking, carrying shopping, gardening, taking the stairs instead of the elevator, and even fidgeting.

Walking, in particular, is one of the “most underrated tools for fat loss,” says Bentley.

“It increases daily energy expenditure without adding a ton of fatigue or interfering with recovery.”

“You don't have to spend hours in the gym to increase your daily energy expenditure,” Campus adds. “Taking the stairs, playing with your children or simply breaking up long periods of sitting all count.”

Your training only works if your body has the opportunity to recover and adapt.

“More training isn't always better, especially when life is already demanding,” says Campus. “Prioritize sleep, rest days, nutrition and lighter sessions when needed.”

Bentley also recommends resting for at least two minutes between challenging sets so you can perform at your best.

Consistent habits over weeks and months matter far more than one meal or one day.

“A meal out, birthday cake, or a weekend treat isn't going to undo weeks of regular training and healthy eating habits,” Campus says. “The goal is balance, not perfection.”

Bentley agrees, urging women to “eat the pizza” and “enjoy dinner with your friends.” Have fun, she says, then simply “get back to your usual routine” without dwelling on it.

It's rare to feel motivated all the time, but you don't need to, say both experts.

Building habits and routines that fit around your life—and sticking with them even when motivation dips—is a much more reliable approach.

“For fat loss, your overall calorie intake matters more than obsessing over whether those calories came from carbs or fat,” says Bentley.

When considering macros, she says to focus on food in this order: calories first, followed by protein and fiber.

Campus agrees. “Energy balance is important, but that doesn't mean calories are the only thing worth considering,” she says. “Protein, fiber, food quality, portion sizes, sleep, and activity all influence how sustainable your diet feels and how well you can stick to it.”

Kate puts together fitness content that covers functional and strength training, cardio, workout challenges, interviews and news. She's often doing gym laundry or listening to music.

Sarah Campus is a highly qualified women's PT, Nutrition Coach, Running Coach, Distance Runner, mum of 3 and founder of LDN MUMS FITNESS.

She's the host of the Soho House Run Club in Chiswick and a STRAVA and TOMMY's marathon coach and ambassador. She specialises helping non-runners get into competitive distance running.

As a fitness and holistic wellness expert, Sarah regularly features on TV and in Magazines, offering tips and advice to keep the whole family healthy and active.

#5110. Bitcoin ETF Investors Head for the Exits After Record Binance Hack

Bitcoin ETF Investors Head for the Exits After Record Binance Hack

The first real test of how ETF investors would react to the largest crypto hack in history came back red.

U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs shed $643.2 million on Friday, according to Decrypt’s ETF tracker, their biggest single-day outflow of October. It was the funds’ first full session since attackers began draining roughly $6.4 billion from Binance at 4:05 p.m. ET Thursday, after U.S. markets had closed. Thursday’s $17.2 million in inflows predated the theft.

The selling erased a four-day, $441 million inflow run, leaving the funds down about $202 million for the week and roughly $516 million for October. Cumulative net inflows since launch slipped to $57.4 billion, with total net assets at $103.2 billion.

Ethereum and Solana ETFs fared worse relative to their size. Ethereum funds lost $155.7 million on Friday, extending a five-day outflow streak to about $431 million and pushing October losses to roughly $1.61 billion. Solana ETFs shed $10.8 million, their fifth straight day of outflows, totaling about $72 million for the week. They logged net inflows on just two days all month.

Spot crypto ETFs hold the underlying asset, such as Bitcoin or Ethereum, and trade on stock exchanges like ordinary shares, giving investors exposure through a regular brokerage account.

Bitcoin funds have drawn $57.4 billion in net inflows since debuting in January 2024, and Ethereum funds have taken in $12.2 billion since launching that July. Because issuers report flows daily and much of the money comes from institutions and financial advisers, the figures have become a closely watched gauge of investor sentiment.

Crypto markets initially appeared to take the Binance hack largely in stride, considering the scale of the theft. Bitcoin fell about 4% to roughly $76,400 on Friday, and more than $1.3 billion in leveraged positions were liquidated. It’s a sharp drop, but a far smaller one than the nearly 11% plunge after a failed attack on Binance in 2018.

Binance has pledged to cover all losses with company funds and an emergency reserve, while Tether and Circle froze more than $2.3 billion in stolen stablecoins. Some Wall Street firms even urged clients to buy crypto-linked stocks on the potential weakness in markets.

By Saturday, Bitcoin had steadied at about $77,520, up 0.5% over 24 hours but down 3.3% on the week. Ethereum traded near $2,251, down 3.9% for the week, and Solana near $96, down 4.5%. Total crypto market cap held at about $2.7 trillion.

Macro headwinds remain in play. The Federal Reserve held rates at 3.75% to 4% on Wednesday, but two officials voted for a hike, and traders had priced odds above 70% for an increase in December.

Traders on Myriad, a prediction market developed by Decrypt’s parent company Dastan, have turned bearish on Ethereum and Solana. They now give Ethereum a 51.3% chance of falling to $1,500 before reaching $3,000, flipping a market that favored the upside at 61.3% a week ago. Solana’s odds of sinking to $40 before hitting $160 rose to 53.7%.

#5109. This Is What Weed Really Did to My Body

How a medical marijuana card works for three real guys.

In November 1996, California became the first state to legalize medical cannabis. (Many) politics and (some) physicians promised us breakthroughs in better health. Thirty years later, how are we really doing? Men’s Health decided to find out in this special report package. Read more here.

THREE REAL GUYS. Three dramatically different mental health conditions. Here’s how a weed card works for them.

THE GUY: TJ, a 47-year-old data insights professional

THE CONDITION: Multiple sclerosis, a chronic autoimmune disease that affects the central nervous system

THE EXPERIENCE: TJ was diagnosed with MS at the age of 25. Doctors prescribed him Rebif, a drug that helped his symptoms but made him depressed. “I was immediately suicidal on that medicine,” he says. Years before medical marijuana was legalized in his state, a friend suggested pot. TJ was desperate, tried weed, and says he felt immediate relief. In 2022, he obtained his medical card. Today, TJ struggles with nerve pain and migraines but says cannabis helps him play volleyball, travel, and stay active. “I don’t know that I would have a productive and happy life if I were not treating myself that way.”

THE GUY: CJ, a 32-year-old postdoctoral researcher of addiction neurobiology

THE CONDITION: Sarcoidosis, an inflammatory disease that causes collections of immune cells, called granulomas, to form in the body. Depending on the location, granulomas can result in fatigue, joint pain, nausea, and swelling. CJ has a rare form located in his brain stem.

THE EXPERIENCE: CJ’s symptoms began in his early 20s, starting with skin issues and leading, eventually, to debilitating nausea. After doctors diagnosed him with sarcoidosis in 2021, he went from using cannabis occasionally to using it daily for nausea control. Through self-experimentation, he learned the combination of cannabinoids that worked the best. Because the ratios were harder to find in OTC products, CJ got his medical card in 2023. “This brought down my overall symptom load a huge amount,” CJ says. Along with an off-label Crohn’s disease medication, medical marijuana has brought him back to 90 percent of his old self, he says.

THE GUY: Jonah, a 41-year-old bookstore manager

THE CONDITION: Generalized anxiety disorder (GAD), a mental health condition that causes excessive fear and worry and interferes with day-to-day functioning.

THE EXPERIENCE: Jonah says he was always labeled “the weird kid.” He ran away at 16 and experienced problems with drugs, alcohol, and homelessness in his early 20s, until his mother convinced him to talk to a therapist, who diagnosed him with anxiety and recommended medical marijuana. After using cannabis for a few weeks, Jonah says, the dark fog that had made him want to act out or isolate himself began to lift. “Conversations with people became a lot easier; decision-making, especially good decisions, became a lot easier.” Sixteen years after that therapy appointment, he’s happily married and life is good, he says.

There’s so much more going on in the world of weed and health. Read more here.

Lauren Vinopal is a writer based in Chicago whose work has been published in GQ, Slate, Mel Magazine, and more.

#5108. Spot bitcoin ETFs see largest daily outflows in four months following record Binance hack

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

US spot bitcoin ETFs shed $643 million on Friday, marking their largest single-day outflow since June 25.

U.S. spot bitcoin Bitcoin (BTC) exchange-traded funds recorded their largest daily net outflows in four months on Friday, following Thursday's $6.3 billion hack at Binance.

According to SoSoValue data, spot bitcoin ETFs saw $643.3 million in net outflows on Friday, marking their largest single-day outflow since June 25. Their outflows for the week ending Oct. 30 amounted to $202.6 million.

Spot Ethereum Ethereum (ETH) ETFs saw $155.9 million in net outflows on Friday, their largest since Oct. 7. The ether funds reported $431.5 million in weekly outflows.

The ETF outflows came after Binance was hit by the largest crypto hack to date. Attackers, whom the FBI has identified as North Korean hackers, drained about $6.3 billion from Binance's hot wallets on Thursday.

The crypto exchange said it recorded roughly $3.64 billion in net outflows in the first nine and a half hours after it began restoring withdrawals on Sunday.

Bitcoin rose 1% over the past 24 hours to trade at $78,491 as of 11:20 p.m. ET Sunday, while ether climbed 1.3% to $2,296, according to The Block's crypto price page.

Jeff Ko, chief analyst of ViaBTC, told The Block that he views the ETF outflows as more of a "sentiment shock."

"Spot ETF holders have no direct exposure to Binance, since the underlying BTC is held with regulated custodians. And in context, a roughly $640 million outflow is actually modest against the total ETF assets," said Ko.

Rick Maeda, head of markets at Laevitas, shared similar views, noting that Friday's outflow was relatively small compared with the ETFs' total net assets of $98.14 billion.

"In scale, the $643 million outflow is still under 1% of spot BTC ETF assets so this was simply a de-risking day, and not capitulation or a loss in institutional confidence," said Maeda.

Still, analysts expect investor caution to persist in the near term.

"Taking a look at a similar precedent of Bybit's Q1 2025 hack, I would expect stabilization or modest outflows in the near term rather than a snap-back as long as the hack can be contained at its current situation," Maeda added.

Dominick John, analyst of Zeus Research, also said that the near-term demand is likely to remain cautious, "with a sustained recovery in ETF inflows dependent on improved market stability, restored liquidity and stronger investor confidence."

The Crypto Fear and Greed Index stood at 37 on Sunday in the fear territory.

"The current sentiment in crypto seems to be one of caution rather than fear," said Maeda. "Markets will be headline-sensitive as traders look for updates regarding the hack as well as the macro calendar such as the U.S. midterms."

Ko also pointed to the midterm elections as another key event.

"BTC has absorbed the hack quite resiliently," said Ko. "Attention is now shifting toward U.S. midterm elections, where the balance of power in Congress could have meaningful implications for how crypto market structure legislation moves forward."

#5106. This Woman Lifted Weights 5 Times a Week for 4 Weeks—Here’s How Her Body Changed

Makari Espe swapped two 20-min weights sessions a week for five 30-min sessions—and documented the results.

If ever you’ve wondered exactly what upping the amount of weight training you do could do for your body, let Canadian YouTuber Makari Espe’s story give you an idea. Already accustomed to 20-minute strength sessions twice a week, she committed to five 30-minute workouts every week for four weeks. The aim? To become “harder, better, faster, and stronger.”

To put her results to the test, she recorded her strength before and after the challenge, from how long she could hold a plank to how many chin-ups and push-ups she could do. Along the way, she also documented the reality of training five days a week on her YouTube channel. By the end of those 28 days, she wasn’t just feeling stronger—she could perform more reps, hold exercises for longer, and lift heavier weights. Here’s what happened.

“My current weightlifting routine is 20 minutes a day, two times per week, but I want to be harder, better, faster, and stronger. So, for the next month, I'm going to lift weights five times a week for 30 minutes.”

“Basically, the idea is to do two big muscle groups per workout so that each muscle group is rested by the time you work it out again and we're not all out here hating our lives. For example, it’s 7:00 in the morning and today we're going to be doing back and biceps. Tomorrow I'll be doing legs and shoulders, and then day three is a rest day.”

“I didn't come up with this routine by myself, by the way. I'm not a personal trainer and I'm very lazy when it comes to planning my own workouts. I wish this was a sponsor, but it's not. I'm just here to tell you that I'm getting all of my workouts from the Peloton app. Woo! This week I'll be doing the beginner 5-day program, and for the next 3 weeks I'll be doing these intermediate programs.”

Espe performed a baseline strength test at the start of her 28-day challenge and the same test at the end of the 28 days. She recorded the following results:

Before:1:30 plank / 1-minute wall sit / 2 chin-ups / 10 push-ups / 25 × 10-lb shoulder presses.

After four weeks:2-minute plank / 2-minute wall sit / 4 chin-ups / 15 push-ups / 25 × 15-lb shoulder presses.

As Espe’s body got used to the change in training frequency, she noticed her soreness increased. That post-workout pain has a name: delayed-onset muscle soreness (DOMS), the stiffness and tenderness that can kick in a day or two after a challenging or unfamiliar session as your muscles repair and adapt.

By week two, she said, “My triceps are killing me more so than any other muscle group this whole challenge so far, my body is sore in many places and I'm very tired.” She added that her hamstrings were “so sore” and her “legs hurt so bad.” By week three, she said, “The place where it hurts right now is my shoulder blades; it feels like somebody hit me with a hammer.”

The DOMS might have intensified, but she did work out an effective salve: active recovery. For Espe, that meant gentle cycling, swimming, and stretching, which helped increase blood flow to the sore areas and to ease the stiffness without adding to her training load.

She said: “It's 7:30 a.m. and I just went for a bike ride and now I'm going to go for a swim. I've been wanting to do that every day this week and I finally did it, and it was glorious. Best morning ever. And now I'm going to go stretch. The swim was amazing for many reasons, but the main one was it was so nice on my sore muscles.”

She didn’t set out with aesthetic goals, and it was her performance that really improved. On week three, she said: “I've crossed the threshold and I’ve found the joy in weightlifting again. I'm doing it because I know I can and I'm super strong. I just feel really strong and that's awesome.” A week later, by week four, she added, “I actually feel even stronger than I did as a teenager.”

As her fatigue increased in those initial weeks, by week three, she decided to take stock of what she was putting into her body—and her energy soared: “I'm putting so much more good fuel into my body which is giving me more energy to perform in the gym,” she said.

As the challenge came to an end, she noticed that her mindset had improved as well as her physical strength. “This is going to sound really cheesy, but it's because not only do I feel physically stronger, but I feel stronger in my brain. Like, I feel like I'm more resilient for having been able to push through all of the self-doubt,” she said.

“I will say that roughly 3 and 1/2 hours every week is a lot of free time to be able to put towards working out, but ultimately it makes me feel so much better outside of the gym, and that's kind of why I do all this.”

“So if you decide to do a challenge like this, absolutely take pride in your physical accomplishments, but please be sure to notice the benefits outside of the gym too.”

As Women’s Health UK’s fitness director and a qualified Pilates and yoga instructor, Bridie Wilkins has been passionately reporting on exercise, health and nutrition since the start of her decade-long career in journalism.

After earning a first-class degree in journalism and NCTJ accreditation, she secured her first role at Look Magazine, where she launched the magazine’s health and fitness column, Look Fit, before going on to become Health and Fitness writer at HELLO!

Since, she has written for Stylist, Glamour, Cosmopolitan, Marie Claire, Elle, The Metro, Runner’s World and Red. Today, she oversees all fitness content across Women's Health online and in print, spearheading leading cross-platform franchises, such as ‘Fit At Any Age’, which showcases the women proving that age is no barrier to exercise.

She has also represented the brand on BBC Radio London, plus various podcasts and Substacks – all with the aim to encourage more women to exercise and show them how. Outside of work, find her trying the latest Pilates studio, testing her VO2 max for fun (TY, Oura), or posting workouts on Instagram.

#5105. FBI Blames North Korea for $6B Binance Hack as Exchange Reopens Withdrawals

FBI Blames North Korea for $6B Binance Hack as Exchange Reopens Withdrawals

The FBI has confirmed that North Korea was behind last week's theft of more than $6 billion from Binance, as the exchange began reopening withdrawals and reported billions of dollars leaving the platform.

In an alert published on Saturday, the FBI said the attack was carried out by North Korean hackers it refers to as "TraderTraitor," confirming the attribution blockchain analytics firms TRM Labs and Chainalysis made on Friday.

The FBI is releasing information advising the Democratic People's Republic of Korea (North Korea) was responsible for the theft of more than $6 billion USD in virtual assets from cryptocurrency exchange, Binance, on or about October 29, 2026. The FBI refers to this specific North… pic.twitter.com/TBUvUFln4n

— FBI Cyber Branch (@FBICyberBranch) October 31, 2026

The FBI, together with the Secret Service and IRS Criminal Investigation, has seen the hackers converting stolen assets mainly into Ethereum, and expects them to launder the funds further through DeFi and instant swap services, the bureau said. It urged node operators, instant exchanges, DeFi services, blockchain analytics firms and other crypto businesses to block transactions involving hundreds of Ethereum addresses, 19 Bitcoin addresses and one Solana address it linked to the theft.

The Secret Service, which is also investigating the theft, said on Sunday that its investigators worked with industry partners to identify and freeze "a significant portion" of the stolen crypto within minutes of the attack. That work continues with the FBI, IRS Criminal Investigation, the U.S. Attorney's Offices for Washington, D.C. and the Central District of California, and the Justice Department's computer crime and national security divisions, the agency said.

The laundering is already well underway. After the attack, the hackers moved more than $500 million on Tron through more than 1,000 addresses, over 3,000 deposits and several Tron-based DeFi protocols, blockchain analytics firm Chainalysis said on Sunday. Most of those staging wallets have now been drained, with funds leaving Tron through cross-chain services including Chainflip, Relay, THORChain and NEAR for dozens of fresh wallets on Ethereum, Bitcoin and other chains, and the launderers have started moving onto Solana, the firm said.

Binance began restoring withdrawals in phases at 1 a.m. ET on Sunday, and all 15 networks and assets in its first phase were available by 7:36 a.m. ET. In the first nine and a half hours, it processed $4.649 billion in withdrawals and $1.01 billion in deposits, a net outflow of about $3.64 billion, the exchange said.

That represents 2.1% of the $171 billion in assets Binance reported in its most recent proof of reserves, from October 28, according to the exchange. "After an initial spike, withdrawal volumes have declined and are stabilising as expected," Binance said. It took a new reserves snapshot on October 31 and expects to publish an updated report on Monday.

As withdrawals began, CEO Richard Teng said Binance had replenished approximately 95% of the affected assets. "Binance will absorb the financial impact of the stolen funds, not our users," he wrote.

Today, we're beginning the phased resumption of withdrawals, taking a careful approach to ensure services return safely and securely.

We've now replenished approximately 95% of the impacted assets, and our commitment remains clear: Binance will absorb the financial impact of the… https://t.co/wKvI7Q4fsT pic.twitter.com/uWFGs3sJJ5

— Richard Teng (@_RichardTeng) November 1, 2026

Binance has since reopened withdrawals on more networks, including Zcash, TON and Sui, and on Monday restored fiat withdrawals in U.S. dollars, UAE dirhams, Vietnamese dong, Philippine pesos and Indonesian rupiah.

"Our top priority right now is to ensure the environment is secure, after which users can withdraw their funds and Binance can resume operations," Binance co-CEO Yi He said on X on Monday, Reuters reported. Binance said it has raised $6 billion to cover the losses from its corporate treasury, an insurance fund and its SAFU emergency fund, according to Reuters, which also reported that Circle had frozen more than $1.1 billion in USDC.

Data from DefiLlama, which tracks the public wallets of exchanges, put Binance's total assets at about $127.6 billion on Monday. More than $6 billion left wallets associated with Binance over 24 hours after withdrawals reopened, CoinDesk reported, citing DefiLlama, though those movements can include transfers between Binance's own wallets. Rival exchanges OKX and Bybit recorded net inflows of about $450 million and $134 million, respectively, during Binance's reopening.

The hack has also rattled institutional investors. Spot Bitcoin ETFs shed $643.2 million on Friday, the first full trading session after the hack, leaving the funds with roughly $516 million in net outflows for October. "A hack at a crypto exchange is bank robbery at the speed of the internet," TRM Labs' Ari Redbord told Reuters.

#5101. ‘We found room for even more orange’: Michael Saylor’s Strategy scoops up another 811 bitcoin for $65.5 million

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Strategy bought another 811 BTC for approximately $65.5 million last week for an average price of $80,846 per bitcoin.

Bitcoin Bitcoin (BTC) treasury company Strategy Strategy, Inc. (MSTR) picked up another 811 BTC for roughly $65.5 million at an average price of $80,846 per bitcoin between Oct. 26 and Nov. 1, according to an 8-K filing with the Securities and Exchange Commission on Monday.

As a result, Strategy's total holdings now stand at 849,327 BTC ($65.9 billion), acquired for a total cost of around $64.1 billion at an average price of $75,451 per bitcoin, and representing more than 4% of bitcoin's total supply cap.

The latest acquisitions used proceeds from at-the-market sales of Strategy's Class A common stock, MSTR, following a pause in acquisitions the week before. Strategy sold 1,178,552 MSTR shares for approximately $164 million during the period, with $18.62 billion worth of MSTR shares left available for issuance and sale under that ATM program, according to the filing.

Strategy also repurchased nearly 2.1 million STRC preferred shares last week for around $208.2 million, using its USD Cash reserve. The company also used $43.4 million from its USD Reserve to fund dividends on its preferred stock.

As of Nov. 1, Strategy's USD Reserve and USD Cash balances stood at $4.79 billion and $132.7 million, respectively, according to the firm.

On Thursday, Strategy posted $19.5 billion in net income for the third quarter, up from $2.79 billion during the same quarter last year, with the rising value of its bitcoin holdings generating a $20.9 billion gain.

"We're at the beginning of the next bitcoin bull market," Executive Chairman Michael Saylor said at the time. "Banks are embracing bitcoin, bitcoin's dominance among digital assets is growing, institutional demand is back, and bitcoin price is 21% above its 200-week moving average. Digital Credit continues to grow, with $16 billion in notional value across ten publicly listed securities."

Meanwhile, Strategy said CFO Andrew Kang will step down on Nov. 5, with current COO Reza Virasteh set to take on the role in January.

Earlier in the week, STRC, STRF, STRK and STRD preferred shareholders approved a change allowing dividends to accrue daily, rather than on the previous fortnightly schedule, with the amendment receiving 99.3% of votes cast.

Analysts at StoneX and TD Cowen said Strategy is in a stronger position to buy more bitcoin after stabilizing its preferred-stock financing and improving its liquidity.

Saylor posted another Strategy bitcoin acquisition tracker chart on Sunday, saying, "We found room for even more orange," indicating the latest acquisition announcement in advance of Monday's filing.

Strategy is the largest of 199 public companies that have some form of bitcoin treasury model. Metaplanet, Tether-backed Twenty One, Strive, and MARA represent the rest of the top five, with 44,000 BTC, 43,514 BTC, 43,000 BTC, and 35,577 BTC, respectively, according to Bitcoin Treasuries data.

Strategy's stock dropped 9.6% last week, closing at $130.51 on Friday, according to The Block's MSTR price page. Bitcoin fell 3.6% over the same period amid the record $6.3 billion Binance hack.

#5099. BTCPay Tells Merchants to Switch Off Servers Amid Hot Wallet Attack Reports

BTCPay Tells Merchants to Switch Off Servers Amid Hot Wallet Attack Reports

BTCPay Server, a free, open-source tool businesses use to accept Bitcoin payments, told operators to switch off their servers on Sunday after reports that attackers were draining the hot wallets connected to them.

"We are receiving reports of hot wallets on BTCPay Servers being swept by unauthorized attackers," the project tweeted at 5:25 p.m. ET. "If you're running an instance, please shut it down (./btcpay-down.sh) until we complete the investigation."

🚨 We are receiving reports of hot wallets on BTCPay Servers being swept by unauthorized attackers.

If you're running an instance, please shut it down (./btcpay-down.sh) until we complete the investigation.

— BTCPay Server (@BtcpayServer) November 1, 2026

About two hours later, BTCPay founder Nicolas Dorier said the reports appeared to involve releases older than version 2.4.2, and that the attacker had logged in to affected servers with administrator access.

Later on Sunday, the project said its investigation had identified an attack vector targeting instances running version 2.4.1, released on July 23, or older. "We have no credible reports of hot wallet drains for users running the latest 2.4.7 release," it said, urging everyone to keep their software updated and asking affected users to send details and logs to its security team.

Some hosting providers, including LunaNode, have shut down BTCPay instances automatically to stop older versions from being exploited, the project said. BTCPay has not said how many servers were affected or how much Bitcoin was taken.

Because BTCPay is self-hosted, each merchant runs the software on their own server, and there is no central operator that can switch instances off. Merchants can choose to run a hot wallet, which stores the wallet's seed on that internet-connected server so it can sign transactions automatically. BTCPay's own documentation labels the feature "DANGER" and warns that it "comes with security implications."

The attacks come days after BTCPay released version 2.4.7 on Thursday as an emergency update fixing one critical and several medium-severity vulnerabilities, which it said were brought to its attention through Anthropic's frontier open-source software scanner. BTCPay has not said whether the attacks exploited any of those flaws.

It is not the first security scare for the project this year. In August, BTCPay warned that a critical vulnerability in all versions before 2.4.2 was being actively exploited, and told users to update or shut down their servers. Supporters of the project later funded a recovery bounty of 10% of any stolen funds returned, capped at 3 BTC.

#5098. The Brooks Running Shoes to Power Your Soccer Training

Find the right pair for sprint work, endurance runs, and active recovery days.

Anyone who’s ever huffed and puffed their way through a pickup soccer game after not playing for a while knows how quickly the sport can expose your conditioning. A match requires repeated bursts of speed with few opportunities to catch your breath, making stamina essential from the first minute through the 90th or beyond.

The average elite soccer player covers about 6.4 miles per game. But even if you’re just a weekend rec-leaguer, cross-training can help you cover more ground without fading late in the match. Running—including sprints, endurance work, and easy jogs—should be a regular part of your routine, and the right shoes can help you get more out of every workout.

Not sure which pair you need? Brooks—a leader in performance running shoes—offers an option for every type of run. Whether you’re doing sprint repeats, heading out for a 10K, or keeping things easy on an off day, here’s how to choose the right model for your workout.

You know what you don’t want to think about when you’re doing sprint drills? Your feet—or at least how your feet feel in your shoes. During workouts built around acceleration and all-out speed, you need a shoe that delivers support, flexibility, and comfort. For the Glycerin Flex, that’s check, check, and check.

Support comes in the form of Brooks’ proprietary nitrogen-infused DNA TUNED midsole foam, which features large cells in the heel to cushion your landings and smaller cells in the forefoot for responsive toe-offs. The combination helps you land comfortably and push off with power as you pick up the pace.

For sprint work, you also need a shoe that moves with you. The Glycerin Flex’s FlexZone technology combines a midfoot flex groove with a podular forefoot design to provide flexibility and support where you need it. A thin layer of sticky rubber on the outsole adds traction as you work on your speed.

You cover plenty of ground every time you take the field for a practice or match, making endurance work essential if you want to keep going through the final whistle. Longer runs can have a place in your conditioning on the days you aren’t wearing your cleats, as long as you build gradually and don’t overdo the mileage.

That’s where the Brooks Ghost Amp comes in. This new take on the brand’s popular Ghost model combines comfort with an energized ride for days when you want to take on a few more miles. Key among its features is SpringRamp forefoot technology, which helps you push off the balls of your feet with a little extra oomph.

Also new is the shoe’s nitrogen-infused DNA HYPE cushioning, which combines two types of foam: lightweight PEBA, which provides high energy return and a springy feel, and EVA, which offers cushioning and stability. Together, they deliver a balance of softness and responsiveness.

Up top, the lightweight warp-knit upper provides breathability and comfort, with enough structure to help secure your foot when you pick up the pace. The eye-catching color options add a little more personality to your training miles.

Recovery days are an essential part of any athlete’s training, especially soccer players. An easy walk or jog can help you stay loose, ease stiffness, and give your mind and body a reset.

But slowing the pace doesn’t mean forgetting about your footwear. On active recovery days, you still want a shoe that provides support and keeps you comfortable. The Glycerin Max 2 does both. Though it’s also built for longer runs, this plush shoe is a natural fit for easier days.

The defining feature of the Glycerin Max 2 is its dramatic GlideRoll Rocker technology. Its sculpted bevel and toe spring help your foot move smoothly from heel to toe. The shoe also features a high stack of DNA TUNED foam, which provides generous cushioning underfoot to keep your legs feeling fresh and protect your body against impact.

Active recovery may not directly put the ball in the net, but the work you do between matches can help you feel ready for the next one. And the right shoe can make that work more comfortable.

#5097. ‘Scromiting’ and Other Super Scary Symptoms of Taking High-THC Weed

Yes, that’s vomiting and screaming at the same time.

In November 1996, California became the first state to legalize medical cannabis. (Many) politics and (some) physicians promised us breakthroughs in better health. Thirty years later, how are we really doing? Men’s Health decided to find out in this special report package. Read more here.

HIGH-CONCENTRATION SYNTHETIC THC products are producing wild reactions in some people. Kevin Gray, MD, a practicing physician and director of addiction sciences at Medical University of South Carolina, helps break them down.

“You experience hallucinations, paranoia, delusions,” says Dr. Gray. “It’s become undeniable looking at the association between teen cannabis use and psychosis that if somebody has some kind of already innate risk for developing a psychotic disorder, there’s something about use of cannabis during this stage of development that [may trigger the onset of] a lifelong disorder.” (The risk of someone developing the condition is less than 1 percent.)

“Paranoia is a symptom of psychosis. Instead of feeling the usual sort of high and calming feeling with cannabis, people feel more detached and more suspicious. Sometimes there will be a very high concentration or some unusual strain that may be more likely to lead to paranoia. The effect can be intense, but it’s transient and wears off with time.”

That’s screaming and vomiting at the same time. “Cyclic vomiting, cannabis-related emesis—I don’t see it that often among my clients, but it happens,” says Dr. Gray. “THC can reduce nausea, but there’s something about the interaction between THC and the systems that control our nausea that goes haywire in some people. We don’t know even how to treat it symptomatically, other than taking showers.” So if you totally misread a gummy label, just call 911—no judgment.

There’s so much more going on in the world of weed and health. Read more here.

Paul Kita is a Deputy Editor at Men's Health, where he has covered food, cooking, nutrition, supplements, grooming, tech, travel, and fatherhood at the brand for more than 15 years. He is also the author of two Men's Health cookbooks, Guy Gourmet and A Man, A Pan, A Plan, and the winner of a James Beard Award.

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The company reported $14.5 billion in total crypto, cash, and, marketable securities as of Nov. 1.

Bitmine Immersion Technologies increased its Ethereum treasury to 6,060,506 ETH after acquiring 8,573 ETH over the past week, the company said in a statement on Monday.

At $2,261 per ETH, the holdings were worth approximately $13.7 billion as of Nov. The haul represents 4.9% of ether's total supply, according to the statement.

Of the total, Bitmine has staked 5,227,037 ETH worth $11.8 billion, representing approximately 86% of its position. The company projects $358 million in annualized staking revenue at current levels, rising to $415 million if its ETH were fully staked.

Beyond ether, Bitmine said it holds 216 BTC, a $180 million stake in Beast Industries, an $82 million investment in Eightco Holdings, and $498 million in cash and marketable securities. Its combined crypto, cash, marketable securities, and other investments totaled $14.5 billion.

Chairman Tom Lee also addressed the recent $6.3 billion Binance hack. He said ETH had declined approximately 4% since the incident was disclosed, compared the decline with an 8% drop on the day the $1.5 billion Bybit theft was announced in February 2025. ETH subsequently fell 20% within a week of the Bybit incident.

"While this is a terrible development, in my view, the relatively muted crypto market reaction is very constructive, particularly compared to the market's response to prior sizable crypto thefts," Lee said.

Lee cited six factors for the muted price reaction, including Binance's ability to cover losses, the absence of decentralized finance protocol failures, and swift industry intervention that limited losses by 50%.

ETH traded at $2,281.41 on Monday, up 0.87% over 24 hours, according to The Block's price page.

Bitmine shares closed Friday at $20.18, down 6.83%.

#5093. Strategy Resumes Bitcoin Buys With $66M Purchase

Strategy Resumes Bitcoin Buys With $66M Purchase

Strategy bought 811 BTC for $65.5 million last week, the company said on Monday, resuming its Bitcoin purchases after a one-week pause.

The Bitcoin treasury firm paid an average of $80,846 per coin between October 26 and November 1, funding the purchases by selling 1,178,552 shares of MSTR stock for $164 million in net proceeds, according to an SEC filing. Of that, $65.5 million went to Bitcoin and $98.5 million to its USD Cash account.

Strategy has acquired 811 $BTC and repurchased $208M of $STRC. As of 11/1/26, we hold 849,327 BTC and $4.9B of USD Assets. $MSTRhttps://t.co/fk7kWJxf3L

Strategy now holds 849,327 BTC, acquired for a total of $64.08 billion at an average of about $75,451 per coin.

The company also bought back 2,092,035 shares of STRC, its variable-rate preferred stock, for $208.2 million, all paid from USD Cash. That is up from $204.9 million the previous week, when Strategy paused its Bitcoin buying for the first time since mid-September. The week before that, it bought 267 BTC for $22.1 million.

USD Cash, the pool Strategy keeps for general purposes including buying Bitcoin, fell to $132.7 million from $242.4 million a week earlier. Its separate USD Reserve, which covers preferred dividends and interest on its debt, stood at $4.79 billion after paying out $43.4 million in dividends. About $748.6 million remains available under its program for repurchasing preferred stock.

Strategy put its combined USD assets at $4.9 billion and its "USD Duration," its measure of how long those assets could cover dividends and interest, at 3.3 years, it tweeted.

The purchases come as STRC moves to a new dividend schedule. Shareholders approved daily dividends on Strategy's four preferred stocks last week, with STRC's first daily record date falling on November 1.

Mizuho Financial Group maintained its Outperform rating and $165 price target on MSTR following Strategy's third-quarter earnings call, pointing to a stronger balance sheet with net leverage down to 2.4% from 8.4%, according to a summary of the note. But analyst Dan Dolev also flagged dilution concerns, noting that Bitcoin per share has fallen 3.7% this year.

Strategy also faces possible removal from MSCI's equity indexes under a rule the index provider finalized in October, which treats companies dominated by a single asset as ineligible. MSCI will publish the list of affected companies on November 11.

Strive has acquired another 124 BTC for approximately $10 million, bringing its total holdings to 43,124 BTC.

Bitcoin treasury company Strive Strive, Inc. (ASST) bought another 124 Bitcoin (BTC) between Oct. 24 and Oct. 30, bringing its total treasury to 43,124 BTC, according to a Monday filing with the Securities and Exchange Commission.

#5089. 30 Years of Legal Weed: Has It Made Us Any Healthier?

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

The very highs and bizarre lows of America on medicinal marijuana.

EARLIER THIS YEAR, during a brief and mercifully abandoned period when I was considering doing stand-up as a hobby, I went to a comedy show in downtown Austin. I was legitimately shocked to find, as part of the two-drink minimum bill of fare, the option for several different flavors of $5 THC seltzers. This wasn’t “take a pull off your vape or smoke a roach in the alley before the show” territory. No, marijuana drinks were on the menu.

In the past few years, my only social activities have been poker, competitive trivia, and going to the movies, all activities people do stoned, but not activities where you can literally order drugs from the bar alongside your chicken wings. The comedy club, on the other hand, considered marijuana products as a profit center.

This was one of those lightly attended weeknight shows where the comedians banter with the audience because they haven’t prepared much material. So, during a Q&A, I brought up the fact that there were THC sodas available to order, in laminate. “Sir, this is 2026,” the host said.

This is where we are now, a full three decades after California legalized medical weed. But is that good for us?

As I write this, it’s been eight-and-a-half years since I quit marijuana, a drug that at one point made pretty much anything—listening to music, shopping for groceries, cleaning the house—fun for me.

But really, I’ve been sober since I stopped using THC, since I was hardly just smoking there at the end. I was vaping, eating, drinking, dabbing, and otherwise gorging myself on what has become an extremely potent, psychosis-inducing drug. And I wasn’t having fun.

My breaking point, which I recounted in my 2020 memoir Pothead, came at Game 2 of the 2017 World Series at Dodger Stadium, where I was so wasted on weed that I had a screaming meltdown in front of security when I found out the ticket I’d bought was fraudulent.

It was a low point, a bottom so deep and so pathetic that I should have quit right there. But I went on for another three weeks before I finally crawled to a Marijuana Anonymous meeting and asked for help.

Now, nearly a decade later, I’ve emerged from my cocoon to find that THC is everywhere. I live in a state where it’s still technically illegal, but you can find some sort of THC variant in most convenience stores and in most bars and nightclubs.

My friend is an attorney who represents the Texas Alcoholic Beverage Commission. He says his client is in a near panic. Sales are down for every beverage except for nonalcoholic mixers and those THC seltzers. THC is the beer, wine, and vodka of the present day, combined.

And so we find ourselves in a bizarre situation where people are drinking way less alcohol because it’s unhealthy for you, but they are instead ingesting THC products—either through soda, gummies, or any number of other concoctions—from unknown manufacturers, with unknown formulas and malleable dosages.

They’re also vaping, which emerging research is showing is kind of awful for our lungs. A University of Michigan study recently showed that teens who vaped cannabis were twice as likely to experience respiratory symptoms like “wheezing or whistling” in the chest than those who vaped nicotine or smoked traditional cigarettes.

Unlike traditional marijuana, this stuff isn’t “from the earth.” Who knows where it’s really from—or where it’s going?

When I quit weed in 2017, and somewhat loudly and publicly announced myself as an addict, I got the requisite amount of sympathy, but also a tremendous amount of pushback. Marijuana isn’t dangerous, people said. It isn’t addictive. It’s actually good for you.

In 2016, Gavin Newsom, then California’s lieutenant governor, spearheaded the legalization push for recreational marijuana. Cannabis sales have produced more than $8 billion in tax revenue for California since retail sales began in 2018, with a decent proportion of that funneled into teen substance-abuse prevention programs. But the state has also seen an increase in children suffering from cannabis poisoning.

It’s been a massive shift, moving from medically administered weed for veterans and cancer patients to powerful THC beverages you can buy at your bodega.

Legalization has created a new frontier of intoxicants without a centralized warning system for users. Data from a 2025 national study of U.S. adults stated that 15 percent of respondents reported daily or near-daily cannabis use.

Among those daily users, 18 percent reported experiencing symptoms consistent with cannabinoid hyperemesis syndrome, a disorder characterized by cyclic, severe nausea, intractable vomiting, and gut pain alleviated temporarily by hot baths or showers.

This translates to an estimated 7.2 million people in the United States alone, many of them teenagers, experiencing these symptoms. That number is probably vastly underestimated, given that fewer than 12 percent of sufferers have received a formal diagnosis.

Welcome to the darker side of legalized weed, a world of cannabis-induced psychosis, “scromiting” (yes, screaming while vomiting), and concoctions like “shatter” or “wax,” which contain up to 90 percent THC, as opposed to the 3 to 5 percent in joints from previous decades.

So I find myself in a somewhat odd situation. I was a relatively early victim of modern weed’s potency, as well as a relatively early unadopter, and a very early voice in the wilderness warning the world that marijuana might not be the answer to all our health concerns. But now I find myself, like a patient waking up from a long coma, in a world where THC is as prevalent as, and cheaper than, whiskey. Yet still, I find myself thinking it should be legal. So which is it? Maybe we still don’t have enough data.

Thirty years since the start of legalization might feel like a long time, but it’s really not. In terms of scientific analysis, we’re still in relatively undiscovered country. If you’re suffering from chronic pain, or dying of cancer, marijuana is a healthier option than, say, oxycodone. But for the rest of us? No one really knows.

The only thing I can be truly sure of is that it’s no longer for me.

#5087. Morning Minute: Binance Hack Leads to Mass Outflows While FBI Opens Probe

Morning Minute: Binance Hack Leads to Mass Outflows While FBI Opens Probe

Morning Minute is a daily newsletter written by Tyler Warner. The analysis and opinions expressed are his own and do not necessarily reflect those of Decrypt. And check out our daily news show 'FOMO HOUR' covering all of the top stories and market action.

💸 The Binance Hack Just Cost Bitcoin ETFs Their Whole Month

Through Thursday, October was a quiet, slightly positive month for Bitcoin ETFs. Then North Korea took $6.4 billion out of Binance five minutes after the closing bell, and Friday’s single session erased the entire month.

To recap the Binance hack, attackers drained roughly $6.4 billion from Binance’s hot wallets across about 10 blockchains, with most of it inside 20 minutes on Thursday afternoon. That’s more than four times the old record, the $1.5 billion Bybit theft in February 2025. Both firms attribute it to TraderTraitor, the North Korea-linked group that also hit Bybit.

The entry point has changed since Friday. Binance first blamed a compromised laptop belonging to a DevOps engineer, then found “additional points of breach.” On Ethereum, the attacker used 50 pre-signed authorizations to turn a Binance hot wallet into a contract that emptied itself—a failure of Binance’s own signing approvals, not of Ethereum. No private keys leaked. The attacker wiped its tooling on the way out.

Friday was the aftermath, and it was bloody. Bitcoin ETFs bled $643.3 million on Friday, the biggest day of the month, ending a four-session buying streak. That one day took October from +$127 million to −$515.9 million. September was +$2.65 billion. The ETH ETFs were worse: −$1.61 billion for October, their worst month since launch, and an 11-session losing streak into the weekend.

Now the biggest players in the U.S. government are reacting. CFTC Chairman Michael Selig told CNBC the hack “proves our exact point.” “The real issue here is that these are unregulated offshore exchanges, and they don’t have the same sorts of safeguards.” He’s pitching a “federal option”—let offshore venues register with the CFTC and meet US cybersecurity standards. He also notes the hole came in “from someone’s laptop,” not from a blockchain. The FBI and Secret Service have also both opened probes.

Binance CEO Richard Teng says the company covers every dollar from corporate funds and SAFU, with $6.34 billion already set aside. CZ: “It hurts, but we survive.” Withdrawals were turned back on over the weekend and over $3.6B has exited so far according to QCP.

So what to watch from here? Monday’s inflows (or outflows) will be telling. If Friday’s bleeding continues to Monday, it’s a signal that the pain might just be beginning. Also, watch Binance. They have to buy roughly $1.3 billion of BTC, ETH, and BNB on the open market and OTC to top off the reimbursement pool—a forced bid underneath a market that just got hit. And then there’s Selig and the FBI; be on the lookout from updates from those agencies as the Binance hack aftermath carries forward.

#5085. Binance completes latest proof of reserves as it works to restore assets, withdrawals after record hack

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Binance is planning another reserves snapshot on Wednesday and said it expects to begin progressively restoring withdrawals for additional networks and assets on Tuesday.

Binance said it has completed a new zero-knowledge proof of reserves snapshot, allowing users holding BTC, ETH, USDT, BNB, SOL, USDC, XRP, USD1, RLUSD and ZEC to independently verify that their balances are backed 1:1.

In its latest recovery update published Monday morning, the exchange said it replenished about 95% of the assets affected by last week's hack with its own funds, with the remaining 5% in less-liquid tokens it is still working to acquire.

"We've completed the latest Proof of Reserves verification, giving users another way to independently check that their assets are backed," Binance Co-CEO Richard Teng wrote in a post on X. "There's still work to do, and we'll continue to communicate our progress openly."

Binance plans another reserves snapshot on Wednesday and said it expects to begin progressively restoring withdrawals for additional networks and assets on Tuesday.

"Computation is required from the snapshot to the updated full zero-knowledge proof, and the generation time depends on the number of tokens," Binance co-founder and co-CEO Yi He said on X. "It is currently estimated that generating the zero-knowledge proof will take about 40 to 50 hours after the snapshot is completed."

In the largest known crypto exploit on record, onchain investigators say that North Korean attackers exploited Binance's internal systems and wallet-signing mechanisms to move more than $6 billion in funds across multiple blockchains. On Sunday, SomaXBT said North Korean hackers laundered about $242 million of the funds stolen from Binance on Tron.

#5083. Weed Addiction Is a Real Thing. The World's Leading Cannabis Researcher Explains Why.

In November 1996, California became the first state to legalize medical cannabis. (Many) politics and (some) physicians promised us breakthroughs in better health. Thirty years later, how are we really doing? Men’s Health decided to find out in this special report package. Read more here.

KEVIN HILL, MD, the guy when it comes to this stuff. He’s the chief of the Division of Addiction Psychiatry at Beth Israel Deaconess Medical Center. We talked to him about all the questions you probably have about weed and addiction.

Dr. Hill: Absolutely. Most people who use cannabis don’t develop a cannabis use disorder or cannabis addiction. But there has always been a subset of individuals who do. Five years ago, the data indicated about 10 to 30 percent of adults develop a cannabis use disorder. Now the published data says 30 to 35 percent. With the rise in potency, we’re seeing a rise in the number of users who are developing problems.

MH: Does cannabis use disorder differ from other addictions?

Dr. Hill: It affects a lot of the same neurotransmitters that other substances of abuse might affect. If you use cannabis, you’re going to have a surge in dopamine. Similarly, if you use opioids, you’re going to have a surge in dopamine, but the scale of it is different. It’s why a lot of medications we use to treat addictions operate on common pathways.

MH: Is there a genetic risk of developing a weed addiction?

Dr. Hill: Yes. The genetic aspects of cannabis use disorder are probably less well developed than for other addictions, but if people have a family history of addiction of any kind, then it makes them more likely to have another substance use disorder, which could be cannabis.

There’s so much more going on in the world of weed and health. Read more here.

#5081. How T7X Is Building the Infrastructure for a New Era of Capital Markets

How T7X Is Building the Infrastructure for a New Era of Capital Markets

Most tokenized stocks aren't natively issued on a blockchain. They begin as conventional shares, sit with a broker or custodian, and are then represented by tokens issued separately. In effect, the market has largely replicated traditional securities structures on-chain instead of rethinking them from the ground up. The token "may track the value of the underlying shares," writes Ivan Kan, CMO of tokenization platform T7X, in an essay shared with Decrypt, "but the token holder is not necessarily the shareholder of record."

While the holder owns the price movement, whether they own the share is another matter altogether. The SEC set out three models in January: issuers tokenizing their own shares, third parties issuing tokens against shares held in custody, and firms issuing a separate security for synthetic exposure.

Writing for Forbes' Technology Council in August, Kan cited Pantera Capital research covering 593 tokenized assets across 11 asset classes, of which 77.6% were what Pantera called "digital wrappers around traditional infrastructure" rather than securities in their own right.

"What does the token legally represent, and who authorized it?" Kan asks, setting out an alternative model that T7X Equities is pursuing, which they dub "native issuance." A security exists because an issuer authorizes it, they argue, and "blockchain does not eliminate those corporate and securities-law requirements."

Under the native issuance model, the token is part of the authorized issuance from the start, running from issuer authorization to investor verification to transfer-agent validation, aiming at "one security, one authorized issuance, one ownership record, one digital representation."

Wyoming, Delaware, and Nevada corporate law recognizes a "certificate token," allowing shares to be represented electronically on a blockchain. T7X is incorporated in Wyoming and structures its issuances to operate within this framework. The blockchain then becomes "part of the infrastructure supporting the capitalization table" instead of being just another instrument layered on top.

The transfer agent keeps the official master securityholder file and the blockchain records transactions, while the legal record of ownership stays where regulators expect it.

In T7X's administrated offerings, that transfer agent is T7X Equities. Transfer agents register with the SEC and hold the authoritative record of who owns a security, processing transfers and maintaining the master file, which puts the company inside the model it is advocating as well as behind it. Its first example is Tranquil Healthcare Fund I, a Tier 2 Regulation A+ offering of up to $75 million that the SEC qualified on June 29. T7X Equities issues tokens representing its shares on Trusted Smart Chain and delivers them to investors' wallets, the company said.

"There's a registered, SEC-regulated gatekeeper that determines who owns which security at any point in time," T7X Chief Executive Pablo Penaloza said at Consensus in Miami earlier this year. "As long as you have a transfer agent involved, there is no way that securities can disappear without trace."

Kan calls the broader category "RWA+." The plus doesn't refer to any single rule, he argues, but the requirement that "compliance and oversight are real," and it rests on three elements. Native issuance creates the token as part of the security's authorized issuance. Integrated compliance writes investor verification and disclosure into the instrument from the start, not as an afterthought. Legally enforceable ownership puts the token holder on the transfer agent's record.

"A receipt is not an asset," Kan wrote in Forbes. "It is a claim wrapped around one, dependent on an intermediary to honor it." Synthetic "PreStocks" tied to SpaceX and Anthropic were never valid claims, he noted, because neither company authorized the transfers. Done properly, he argues, "ownership, compliance and settlement are the same fact."

Whether a token carries the rights of a share is becoming the dividing line elsewhere, too. The SEC's new innovation exemption covers tokenized stocks carrying dividends and voting rights while excluding price-tracking synthetics, and Nasdaq's arrangement with Kraken turns on tokens conveying the same voting rights as ordinary shares.

Penaloza pins the near-term opportunity on companies raising under $100 million, usually between $50 million and $75 million, through Regulation A offerings.

Regulation A+, Kan argues, suits this model because it permits public solicitation and non-accredited participation while requiring audited financials and continuing disclosure. Most tokenized assets rely instead on Regulation D placements, limited to accredited investors. Reg A also opens the door, Penaloza notes, to retirement accounts. Unregistered securities can legally sit in a self-directed IRA, but many brokers won't hold them and most investment advisers steer clients away. "The money is sitting in the older generations' retirement accounts," he said, and a registration attached to the investment is what makes an individual retiree comfortable holding it.

For investors already holding these securities, the benefit is an exit. Shareholders in Reg A and Reg D placements typically receive a paper certificate and then "have to go around and find somebody who will want to give them liquidity," Penaloza said. A native digital security can trade on a secondary market, he said, settling wallet to wallet without a custodian in between.

Giving Reg A and Reg D holdings somewhere to trade would, he argued, "incredibly speed up not only the rate of capital formation, but also the liquidity in the market." Within five years, he predicts, "the total tokenized portfolio will far surpass the existing market capitalization of what is currently trading on all the public markets."

To find out more about T7X’s real-world asset tokenization platform, head to t7x.com.

#5080. What Happens To Your Body When You Cut Back—But Don’t Quit—Alcohol?

If you’ve spent any time on social media recently, your feed is probably packed with people talking up how amazing they feel without alcohol in their lives. But while a growing body of research has found that no amount of alcohol is technically good for you, it’s understandable to still want to enjoy the odd glass of wine or cocktail with friends.

There are a few studies out there on the potential health impacts of avoiding alcohol, but what happens if you just cut back? An all-or-nothing approach doesn’t work for some people, so it’s fair to wonder if you’d still be getting health perks by going this route.

We tapped two doctors who study the impact of alcohol on the body to learn more.

Meet the experts: Megan Strowger, PhD, a post-doctoral research associate at University at Buffalo; Emily A. Gordon, MD, MPH, assistant professor of medicine and program director of Addiction Medicine Fellowship at Rutgers New Jersey Medical School

You’ll still get plenty of health perks if you just have the odd drink, says Megan Strowger, PhD, a post-doctoral research associate at University at Buffalo who recently studied the impact on the body of cutting out alcohol for a month. In fact, she says cutting back can have a “profound impact” on your health, especially if you’ve been a heavy drinker in the past.

Strowger points to an older clinical trial that found people had lower blood pressure, better liver function, and reported a better quality of life within four months of cutting back on their drinking. That shows that “even a moderate reduction in consumption can lead to meaningful improvements for both the body and mind,” Strowger says.

It’s not a bad idea to leave room for a little alcohol in your life if you enjoy it and don’t struggle with alcohol use disorder, says Emily A. Gordon, MD, MPH, assistant professor of medicine and program director of Addiction Medicine Fellowship at Rutgers New Jersey Medical School. “There is a benefit to having a glass of wine to relax and enjoy time with friends, as long as it’s not chronic use,” she says. “The mental health benefits need to be weighed along with the physical.”

There is no hard and fast number, but there is some general guidance to consider. “The World Health Organization categorizes drinking into different ‘risk levels,’ and research shows that moving down just one or two of these levels can lead to significant physical health improvements and fewer alcohol-related complications a year later,” Strowger says.

Gordon points out that there are healthy drinking limits in place (no more than two drinks a day for men and one drink a day for women). “But they are probably higher than they should be,” she says. “As long as you’re moderating your intake, it’s likely healthy.”

If you really want to focus on health, Gordon suggests just trying to minimize how much you drink. “Ultimately, the healthiest approach is to keep consumption as low as possible, as there is no level of use that is considered entirely risk-free,” she says.

You may be just fine deciding you’re only going to drink here and there, but some people prefer a little more guidance. If you fall into the second camp, Gordon recommends thinking about when you tend to drink. “Is it to ease a little anxiety, socially, or out of boredom?” she says. “Then, try to think of healthier things that can be substituted.” That might mean incorporating daily stress-reducing walks into your day, taking up a new hobby, or diving into all the NA options out there to find something you enjoy that you can sip while you socialize with friends.

Tracking how often you drink (including the type and number of drinks you have) can be helpful, too, Strowger says. “This habit is associated with sustained reductions in drinking even a month later,” she says.

If you respond well to clear boundaries, Strowger recommends giving yourself a specific limit on drinks you’ll have in each sitting or per week. “However, it is important to practice self-compassion,” she says. “If you don't always hit your targets, simply carry on and try again during the next week or occasion.” It can even be helpful to practice ways to politely decline when someone offers you a drink after you’ve hit your personal limit, she says.

Ultimately, just do your best. “In general, the less you drink, the better,” Gordon says. “But we do need to recognize the importance of socializing and spending time with friends.” Basically, don’t feel bad about catching up with your crew over a glass of wine. It’s unlikely to hurt your health—and could even give your mental health a boost.

DTCC's permissioned service targets different markets than Securitize's public-blockchain tokenization, StoneX analysts said, keeping a buy rating on SECZ.

The Depository Trust & Clearing Corporation's (DTCC) new tokenization service validates rather than threatens Securitize's business model, according to StoneX analysts, who argue that the two platforms serve different markets.

While DTCC's permissioned service is initially limited to certain securities, participants and blockchain networks, Securitize (SECZ) focuses on public-blockchain tokenization, including tokenized funds, equities and assets that can be used in decentralized finance.
