#5077. This Is Your Brain on Weed. It’s Actually Kind of Amazing.

In November 1996, California became the first state to legalize medical cannabis. (Many) politics and (some) physicians promised us breakthroughs in better health. Thirty years later, how are we really doing? Men’s Health decided to find out in this special report package. Read more here.

WHEN YOU USE weed, your brain kind of goes all over the place. You likely know about how marijuana can chill you out, make you forget yourself, and induce the munchies. But there are other fascinating ways that the drug can alter your mind.

When THC binds to receptors in your hippocampus, the compound disrupts signals and impairs memory. That’s why you may forget what you were saying mid-sentence or where you put your keys when stoned.

THC also binds to receptors in the amygdala, which can help you feel chill when high—and paranoid or anxious when you’re too high.

The back of the brain, the cerebellum, is important for balance, motor skills, and time perception. When THC gets involved, you might feel clumsier and as if time is slowing.

THC disrupts the prefrontal cortex, which oversees critical thinking, problem-solving, and processing information from your senses. As a result, you’re going to find it harder to multitask, feel more impulsive, and zone out.

THC also stimulates the release of feel-good dopamine in the nucleus accumbens. If you’re a chronic, heavy user, cannabis can dull dopamine response and potentially rewire circuitry within the nucleus accumbens, making you more susceptible to addiction.

Your prefrontal cortex and limbic system contribute to imaginative ideas. THC can bind to receptors in these regions and may reduce self-critical noise, allowing for greater free expression.

Scientists believe that the anterior cingulate cortex is central to the perception of pain, and THC seems to dampen its ability—in a good way.

There’s so much more going on in the world of weed and health. Read more here.

#5075. Bitmine Buys More Ethereum as Tom Lee Calls the Binance Hack Bullish

Bitmine Buys More Ethereum as Tom Lee Calls the Binance Hack Bullish

Bitmine Immersion Technologies kept its weekly Ethereum buying streak intact, adding 8,573 ETH worth roughly $19 million, while chairman Tom Lee used the record Binance hack to argue that crypto has grown more resilient, not more fragile.

The NYSE-listed company said Monday its holdings now total 6,060,506 ETH, worth roughly $13.7 billion at a reference price of $2,261 per coin.

That represents 4.9% of Ethereum’s 122.2 million token supply, leaving Bitmine, by its own count, 99% of the way to its goal of owning 5% of the network, a target it brands the “Alchemy of 5%,” reached in just 15 months. The firm has bought Ethereum every week since launching its treasury strategy on June 30, 2025.

Most of Lee’s commentary focused on last week’s roughly $6.4 billion theft from Binance, which the FBI has attributed to North Korea. Lee called it “a terrible development” but argued the market’s “relatively muted” reaction was “very constructive.” (Disclosure: Tom Lee is one of several investors in Dastan, Decrypt’s parent company.)

Lee noted that when North Korea’s Lazarus-linked hackers drained $1.5 billion from Bybit in February 2025, Ethereum fell 8% that day and 20% within a week, whereas ETH slid just 4% after the Binance breach, despite the theft being roughly four times larger.

Lee laid out six reasons for the muted response: Binance’s ability to cover all customer losses, the absence of a cascade of DeFi failures like the one that followed Bybit, swift industry action that cut the potential loss in half, the FBI naming North Korea within two days rather than the months such attributions have taken historically, the fact that the exploit was a “corporate infiltration” of Binance’s infrastructure rather than a blockchain flaw, and the limited price downside itself, which he read as a sign a “crypto bull market is underway.”

Taken together, he said, the episode shows the industry is “increasingly resilient and more capable of absorbing these nation-state assaults.”

Counting cash, other tokens and what it calls “moonshot” investments, Bitmine’s total holdings stood at $14.5 billion as of Nov. 1, including 216 Bitcoin, $498 million in cash and marketable securities, a $180 million stake in Beast Industries and an $82 million position in Eightco Holdings, which it bills as a rare public proxy for indirect OpenAI exposure.

Bitmine remains the world’s largest Ethereum treasury and the second-largest crypto treasury overall, trailing only Michael Saylor’s Strategy, which the release said holds about 848,517 BTC.

Strategy recently resumed its own Bitcoin buying with a $66 million purchase after a brief pause.

Bitmine has 5,227,037 ETH, about 86% of its holdings, staked through its MAVAN platform, with projected annualized staking revenue of roughly $358 million.

#5072. Get To Know The NWSL Teams: All About The Women's Professional Soccer League And Current Rankings

Everything to know about the National Women’s Soccer League before the playoffs start next week.

Big news in women’s soccer: The National Women’s Soccer League just reached a major settlement with the NWSL Players Association to drop restrictive qualifications from its "high impact player" rule. Removing this so-called "Rodman rule" opens the door for any players in the league to get a seven-figure salary starting January 1, 2027.

Under the previous guidelines—which remain in play for the rest of this season—only about 100 players met the requirements for the NWSL's highest pay tier. This new agreement allows any player to be designated as a "high impact player," with 12 percent of their salary counting against the team's salary cap, per ESPN.

This new rule adjustment happened after the league previously rejected a high-paying contract proposal between the Washington Spirit and Trinity Rodman. Under the existing rule, the NWSL lost star American players Alyssa Thompson and Naomi Girma to European teams, where they received more lucrative offers, ESPN reports. Rodman has also received large offers from European teams—the rule change may encourage her to stay in the U.S.

Pretty much everything about women’s soccer is booming. The league has a media deal with ESPN, CBS, Prime Sports, and Scripps’ ION network, reportedly worth $60 million a year, Reuters reports. The NWSL called it the "largest media investment in women's sports history." NWSL players are bonafide stars in their own right, with big names like Sophia Smith, Rodman, and Mallory Swanson earning lucrative sponsorship deals, too.

So, if you're a newbie to the sport (or just looking to brush up ahead of the playoffs, which begin November 6), here's everything to know about the NWSL teams.

There are currently 16 NWSL teams. Each team is allowed a minimum of 22 players and a maximum of 26 players on their rosters, per Soccer Wire.

And here’s a look at the 2026 season rankings, per the NWSL:

The regular season ends on November 1, and the quarterfinal round starts on November 6. You can expect a new NWSL champ to be crowned on November 21.

There's a large range in salaries for the NWSL, but like everything else in the sport, they're on the rise. At the beginning of this year, the NWSL increased the salary cap to $3.7 million. By comparison, the cap was $1.375 million in 2023 and $2.75 million in 2024, per CBS.

There is a little leeway with the cap, based on the rules that stand this second. Each team can exceed the salary cap by up to $1 million for "high-impact players," which are basically the best of the best. (NWSL has specific requirements listed out, including plenty of awards, playing time, and being selected to the USWNT. Again, this will change for next season) Only a minimum of 12 percent of the high-impact player's base salary counts toward the team's salary cap.

The highest-paid player is Catarina Macario of the San Diego Wave FC, who signed a multi-year contract worth just under $8 million through 2030, per CBS Sports.

The women are paid drastically less than men in the American Major League Soccer (MLS), however. For comparison, the MLS' highest-paid male soccer player is Lionel Messi, who makes about $28.3 million per year.

The NWSL's top players typically make about $1 million to $2 million per year, but the minimum salary for the league is $48,500.

The list of team owners in the NWSL may be as exciting as the games themselves.

Los Angeles' Angel City is an expectedly star-studded club, with owners including Natalie Portman and Reddit co-founder Alexis Ohanian. #5071. Is This the Best Thanksgiving Turkey Recipe Ever?

A 5-Ingredient Brine. Cajun Rub. Two Hours in a Smoker. Perfection.

BACK IN 2021, D’Andre Carter was maxed out. The fine-dining chef had opened Soul & Smoke in Chicago, a barbecue-based food truck, and it incinerated his free time, which caused his weight to tick upward.

Then came the hypertension diagnosis, a wake-up call that he needed to act fast. He started jogging and eventually worked up to 60-minute runs five days a week, plus bodyweight exercises.

His diet now is loaded with lean protein (salmon, chicken) and nutrient-dense smoothies. He’s down 20 pounds from 220, his hypertension is in check, and he’s motivated. “Living healthy gives me the stamina to manage a busy life, be present for my family, and lead in the kitchen,” Carter says.

That means cooking well, for everyone, and especially on Thanksgiving, when he turns to this way-more-delicious holiday feast.

“This is my go-to method for a juicy, flavorful whole turkey—perfect for holidays or any gathering,” says Carter. “It’s all about the prep, the brine, and that smoke.”

1. In a large pot, stir 8 quarts of warm water with 1 cup of kosher salt, plus the sugar, onion, garlic, and thyme, until dissolved. Chill till cool, submerge the turkey in the liquid, and brine in the fridge overnight.

2. Remove the turkey from the brine, pat it dry, and rub all over with the olive oil. Season with the rub and 1 Tbsp coarsely ground black pepper.

3. Preheat a kamado smoker (like a Big Green Egg) or arrange a grill for indirect cooking at 350°F. Add the wood chunks. Place the turkey on the grill, breast side up, tucking in the wings underneath. Smoke, lid closed, for 1 hour, open the lid, baste the bird with the butter, and close the lid. Smoke until the internal temp reaches 170°F in the thickest part of the thigh, another 1 to 2 hours. Let the turkey rest for 30 minutes before carving. Feeds 10, with leftovers

The key to amazing barbecued anything is in the spice blend. This one works great on turkey, but also chicken and vegetables.

In a bowl, mix ¼ cup smoked paprika, 2 Tbsp kosher salt, 2 Tbsp garlic powder, 2 Tbsp onion powder, 2 tsp ground black pepper, 2 tsp dried thyme, 2 tsp dried oregano, 1 tsp cayenne pepper (adjust to taste), 1 tsp ground white pepper, and 1 tsp brown sugar. Makes roughly ¾ cup

#5070. Kim Jong-Un’s sister rejects FBI claims linking North Korea to record $6 billion Binance hack: reports

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

On Monday, Yonhap News Agency reported that hackers from North Korea were not behind the over $6 billion hack of Binance, citing the sister of North Korea's leader Kim Jong-Un.

A North Korean official has denied the country was behind the largest crypto hack on record, despite onchain investigators and the FBI attributing the $6 billion exploit to North Korean hackers.

On Monday, Yonhap News Agency reported that Kim Yo-jong, the sister of North Korean leader Kim Jong-Un and a senior government official, rejected the FBI's assessment that North Korea was responsible for last week's Binance hack.

"The FBI is trying to brand the DPRK as a 'cyber criminal state', claiming that it obtained information that a hacking organization 'supported' by the DPRK was involved in the incident," Kim said in a statement, according to Yonhap.

The FBI did not immediately respond to a request for comment from The Block.

Ahead of the attack last week, an attacker deployed and tested malicious code on three wallets before later executing the attack on Binance on Thursday. On Ethereum, the attacker used EIP-7702 to turn a Binance hot wallet into a sweeper controlled by the attacker. It prepared 50 pre-signed authorizations to ensure one would remain valid when executed, according to TRM Labs.

Three firms, including TRM Labs, attributed the attack to North Korean bad actors. Then, over the weekend, the FBI confirmed that North Korea was responsible for the hack. In its release, the FBI referred to this specific North Korean malicious cyber activity as "TraderTraitor."

Earlier on Monday, Binance published its first set of proof of reserves and said it replenished about 95% of the assets affected by the hack with its own funds.

#5064. Brazil's Largest Exchange Mercado Bitcoin Halts Withdrawals After $40 Million Hack

Brazil's Largest Exchange Mercado Bitcoin Halts Withdrawals After $40 Million Hack

Brazil's largest crypto exchange, Mercado Bitcoin, suspended deposits and withdrawals on Monday after identifying a security incident in one of its systems in the early hours of the morning. The exchange said no customer lost money and no data leaked.

"We identified and quickly contained a security incident in one of our systems early this morning," Mercado Bitcoin said in a Portuguese-language statement on X. "As a preventive protection measure, deposits and withdrawals are temporarily suspended."

Blockchain security firm Hypernative flagged more than $40 million in suspicious transfers that may be linked to the incident, according to a preliminary analysis that spans at least 10 blockchains, including Bitcoin, Ethereum, Solana, and Tron. Mercado Bitcoin has not confirmed that figure and has not disclosed how the attackers got in.

In a follow-up post, the exchange published the destination addresses it identified on five networks: EVM chains (blockchains built to run the same code as Ethereum), Bitcoin, Solana, Stellar, and Tron. It said the addresses were released only so others can identify and track the funds on-chain.

Reforçamos que:• Não houve nenhum prejuízo aos clientes;• Não houve vazamento de dados.

Como medida preventiva de proteção, depósitos e saques…

— Mercado Bitcoin (@MercadoBitcoin) November 2, 2026

Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino said in a post on X that the stablecoin issuer has frozen more than 4.5 million USDT connected to the incident.

USDT is a dollar-pegged stablecoin, and Tether can blacklist the addresses holding it so the tokens cannot be moved, though a freeze does not by itself return the funds to their owner.

The incident comes days after hackers drained more than $6 billion from Binance on October 29, a theft the FBI attributed to North Korea's TraderTraitor group. Binance has since reopened withdrawals and said it replenished about 95% of the affected assets. Mercado Bitcoin has not linked the two incidents and has not said who is behind its own.

Deposits and withdrawals at Mercado Bitcoin remain suspended, and the exchange has not given a date for resuming them. It said it will post regular updates on X.

#5060. 4 Workout Strategies This Over-50 Trainer Uses to Keep Building Muscle

Use his guidance so you can train hard and hurt less.

THE MUSCLE-BUILDING NARRATIVE after age 50 isn’t pretty. According to doomers online, your testosterone levels are falling, your energy levels are in massive decline, and everything seems to throw your back out.

But contrary to popular belief, you can still muscle up. And Bryan Krahn, 53 years old and as strong as he’s ever been, is proof. The trainer, who created MH’s Max Muscle at 50 workout program, still works out four or five days a week and has maintained a muscular 210-pound physique..

That doesn’t mean he trains exactly like he did when he was in his 20s. Instead, over the last three decades, he’s made a series of adjustments, shifting his fitness priorities (much less bench pressing) and experimenting with new gym ideas. All that trial-and-error helped Krahn develop these four critical post-50 training principles, which will allow you to push your muscle-building limits for decades to come without crushing your body. He discussed these ideas (and much more) on MH’s Strong Talk podcast.

LIKE MOST GUYS, Krahn relied on dumbbells and barbells for years. But about a decade ago, those exercises started to hurt his joints. He has since shifted his focus to training that minimizes his risk of getting hurt. He is quick to note this isn’t about being overly cautious—avoiding injury is not the same as training scared.

Part of the fix for Krahn involved taking a little bit of time off. The other part: He headed to a section of the gym that guys often forget about: the machine area. Because yes, dumbbells and barbells and kettlebells seem to build more “functional” strength (so the narrative goes). But as you get older, those tools don’t always work as well with your joints. “Within four or five months, I have no pain, and I’m strong on these machines,” Krahn says. “Cables are indispensable as you get older.”

Machines allow you to focus on doing movements along very fixed tracks, and that lets you concentrate on generating the maximum amount of force with your muscles. That’s a key mechanic for growth. “And if you’re a beginner, generating that force isn’t always easy with free weights,” says MH fitness director Ebenezer Samuel, C.S.C.S. “Inserting one machine exercise in your routines near the end can insure you get a chance to push your limits just a bit.”

Two machines to consider: A cable row machine, which blasts your back, and the leg press machine, which ensures you can challenge hamstrings, glutes, and quads with relatively heavy load.

A bonus: Experimenting with machines just may reignite your passion for the gym, too. Krahn says the machines allow him to enjoy his workouts—much like he did in his 20s. “I love going to the gym again,” he says.

FROM BARBELL BENCH presses to back squats to barbell deadlifts, some lifts in the gym are so popular and widespread, they might feel mandatory. But no—Krahn says you don't have to commit to any of them.

Krahn learned that when training arms. Yes, he loved skull crushers, a triceps exercise that can be notoriously tough on the elbows. But he swapped in triceps pressdowns and still built the muscle he was chasing. He did the same with his biceps work, swapping out supinated dumbbell biceps curls for hammer curls. "As soon as something feels off," he says, "let's try a variation."

Exercise physiologist and strength coach Dr. Pat Davidson agrees. Most lifts that guys think are mandatory can easily be replaced. "So many people just get emotionally attached to an exercise," Davidson says. "Don't marry an exercise."

Krahn says machines are especially valuable here, too. "It's so difficult to get hurt (on machines)," he says. "You can kind of unleash that side of yourself and really push into failure."

Major lesson: The moment you feel pain in your joints, consider finding a substitute lift for the exercise that's caused you pain. Don't wait.

THESE DAYS, KRAHN thinks about building more than muscle. He also wants to maintain his body’s ability to be powerful. What does it mean to be powerful? Muscles produce force—but they also need the ability to produce that force very quickly, which happens whenever you jump, hop, or begin to run.

And sure, plyometric exercises can train power. But once you’re in your 50s, your body can’t always absorb that pounding. That’s why Krahn loves sled pushes. He uses the move as a warmup, especially on upper body days when he’s focused on a variety of push activities. Pushing a weighted sled is a move that can build glutes, quads, and hamstrings, and light up your core. It also quietly trains something called triple-extension, the act of straightening your knee, hip, and ankle joints all at once—which is a key to running and jumping.

The best part of a sled push, Davidson says: You get to build power without much injury risk. “Anytime you’re in a forward lean acceleration posture, it's much safer,” Davidson says. “It’s almost a Smith machine of running.” Consider doing two to three sets of 30-second sled pushes to start your next workout.

SHORT ON TIME? If Krahn has only 15 minutes in a day, he’ll walk. It’s a non-negotiable in his life. “I get a lot done in that 15 minutes,” he says. “I don’t lift weights every day, but I walk every day.”

There are a lot of reasons to add walking into your daily routine. For Krahn, walking gives him the mental reprieve to step away from his responsibilities and keep his mind refreshed. Walking also keeps your body moving, limiting your sedentary time.

At 50, it’s not always about going hard or pushing or limits. Walking keeps your body moving in a joint-friendly manner, even when you're fatigued (while sneaking in some light calorie-burn, too). Daily walking also helps to lower the risk of heart disease, a concern at any age but especially for older adults.

“Right now, that’s the number one killer in the US,” Mario Mejia, PT, DPT, physical therapist at Next Play Physical Therapy & Performance, previously told MH. “Heart disease can happen anytime but as we age, physiologically there are differences in the heart, lungs, and bone structure.

Above all, Krahn's tips provide a template for how to keep building muscle and lose fat into your 50s and beyond. And Krahn's full Max Muscle at 50 program delivers even more guidance. Check it out here.

Jocelyn Solis-Moreira, MS is the associate health & fitness for Men's Health and has previously written for CNN, Scientific American, Popular Science, and National Geographic before joining the brand. When she's not working, she's doing circus arts or working towards the perfect pull-up.

#5059. Elon Musk Mocks Sam Altman Over Claim Home PCs Are Wasting RAM

Elon Musk Mocks Sam Altman Over Claim Home PCs Are Wasting RAM

Elon Musk has mocked OpenAI CEO Sam Altman for arguing that the world's limited supply of computer memory would be better used in AI data centers than in people's home computers.

Altman made the comments in an interview with the YouTube channel Cheeky Pint, while discussing the shortages of memory and other components caused by the AI boom.

"A lot of people want local inference, and somehow they assume that the problem is solved now because we're going to have open-source models," Altman said, referring to running AI models on a user's own hardware. "But from like a global allocation perspective, actually, it's quite terrible to have everybody have a bunch of the world's limited supply of DRAM sitting in their own computer and being used 5% of the time."

"You want maximum utilization in a cloud if you're trying to deal with this very supply-constrained environment," he added. "And somehow, many people's intuition is the opposite of that."

Musk responded on X on Sunday with a meme showing Hans Landa, the Nazi officer played by Christoph Waltz in the film Inglourious Basterds, captioned: "You are withholding DRAM from the cloud, are you not?" "The people deserve to have their RAM too!" Musk wrote alongside the image.

The people deserve to have their RAM too! pic.twitter.com/q6dwauALw4

In the interview, Altman blamed the shortage partly on memory makers' caution. For years, a DRAM producer "would invest $20 billion of CapEx to make $19 billion," he said, and companies that nearly went bankrupt building too many factories in past cycles have been slow to expand, even as they now stand to generate huge profits.

Altman said he would like to see prices for energy, chips and the rest of the supply chain fall. "If it doesn't go down, I'll be very sad," he said, adding that for prices to fall, supply needs to grow.

The jab is the latest in a long-running feud between the two men, who co-founded OpenAI with others in 2015 before Musk left in 2018.

Musk sued OpenAI and Altman in 2024, accusing them of abandoning the organization's founding nonprofit mission. In May, a federal jury in California rejected the $150 billion lawsuit, finding OpenAI, Altman and president Greg Brockman not liable on all claims. OpenAI's lawyers had argued that the suit was motivated in part by competition, after Musk launched rival AI company xAI in 2023.

#5055. Raw Denim vs. Regular Denim: Which Is Actually Worth Buying?

Here’s what to know about both kinds of jeans.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

I COME FROM the denim era where streetwear juggernauts like FUBU and Rocawear reigned supreme, along with mall favorites like Abercrombie & Fitch and Hollister. I was in my preteens rocking all these clothing brands and you couldn’t tell me their baggy-stacking-at-the-shoe jeans weren’t the flyest on the market. Since I began working as a fashion editor, I’ve practically worn every pair of jeans in the denim universe. Slim, relaxed, stretch, washed, distressed—you name it, I’ve probably tried it. I’ve even tried my hand at denim-making in Japan, the mecca of old-school, high-quality denim, where I produced my own pair of selvedge jeans. Denim is one of those rare items in a guy’s wardrobe that never goes out of style.

And after years of being in the menswear space, I’ve gotten familiar with the nuances that distinguish one kind of denim from the next. Two worth knowing are regular denim and raw denim. Raw denim usually looks darker, stiffer, and more uniform in color, while regular denim has a softer feel and a more broken-in appearance with visible fading or whiskering from factory washing and finishing. Those differences also affect how each pair feels when you first put it on and how it changes over the years. Here’s what to know about both and which one deserves a spot in your rotation.

Regular jeans are usually made by weaving cotton yarn into denim, then cutting fabric into patterns and sewing them together to create the finished garment. These jeans have already gone through washing or finishing treatments to soften the fabric and give them that broken-in look, so you don’t have to spend weeks waiting for them to feel comfortable. If I want to throw on a pair of jeans and get on with my day, this is the kind I’m reaching for. I also like that you can find them in practically every fit and wash, whether you’re after a clean, dark indigo pair or something faded and lived-in. Most everyday options from brands like Levi’s and Wrangler run roughly $40 to $100, while designer and premium styles can cost considerably more. For me, prewashed denim makes the most sense when you want that comfortable feel from the jump. There’s nothing wrong with skipping the break-in period and getting straight to the good part.

I like raw denim because you get to see how jeans change the more you wear them. Unlike prewashed jeans, raw denim typically hasn’t been washed after the dyeing and manufacturing process, so it typically arrives stiff, dark, and pretty rigid. One thing to watch out for, though, is dye transfer. Indigo can rub off onto lighter-colored clothes, shoes, and other materials, including suede. I’ve had this happen before, so I'd be mindful of what you wear with a fresh pair. But that’s part of the appeal for denim enthusiasts. As you wear it, the fabric softens and develops fades and creases based on how you move, sit, and live in your jeans. You’re essentially breaking them in yourself, which makes the finished look feel uniquely yours. Prices can start around $60 to $100 for entry-level options and climb to $150 to $300 or more for premium denim, particularly Japanese-made styles. I’ll admit, the break-in period takes some patience, but if you enjoy watching your jeans evolve over time, raw denim is worth considering.

Raw denim and selvedge denim get lumped together all the time, but they actually refer to different things. Raw denim describes the fabric’s finishing state: It’s typically sold without a post-production wash that softens the fabric or creates a faded look. Selvedge, on the other hand, refers to how the denim is woven, creating a clean, self-finished edge you can often spot by rolling up the cuff. You can have raw selvedge denim, washed selvedge denim, or raw denim that isn’t selvedge at all. Having made my own pair of selvedge jeans in Japan, I’ve seen firsthand how the construction differs. Once you know what to look for, the distinction is pretty easy to spot.

At the end of the day, I don’t think you need to drop $300 on raw denim to dress well. After years of working in menswear, I’ve learned that a higher price tag doesn’t automatically make something the right choice for your wardrobe. I’ve seen guys look just as good in an affordable pair of Levi’s as they do in premium Japanese denim. What matters more is finding comfortable jeans that fit your body and work with the clothes you already own. I’m always going to appreciate the quality craftsmanship behind a great pair of raw denim, but there’s no single right way to wear jeans. I think every guy should have one dependable pair, whether that’s raw or prewashed. But ultimately, like most things in style, it comes down to personal preference—and knowing what works for you.

#5052. Nvidia and Nike Lead Biggest Wave Yet of Tokenized Stock Objections

Nvidia and Nike Lead Biggest Wave Yet of Tokenized Stock Objections

Even Nvidia doesn’t want its shares on OKX and ICE’s planned tokenized stock venue.

The chipmaker is among 12 new companies listed as objecting issuers in an updated notice from OKXICE TSV dated Friday. The others are Costco, Nike, Micron, CrowdStrike, Palo Alto Networks, Reddit, Hewlett Packard Enterprise, Corning, Bloom Energy, IREN, and USA Rare Earth. The additions bring the total to 50 companies.

It’s the largest single batch yet. The list stood at 38 in the venue’s Oct. 28 notice, when Walmart and Eli Lilly joined, and at nine when the first objectors appeared on Oct. 15. It has more than quintupled since.

The newcomers span chips, cybersecurity, retail, and energy. Nvidia, Micron, and Corning join hardware peers such as AMD, Intel, and Broadcom, while CrowdStrike and Palo Alto Networks bring cybersecurity heavyweights to the list. IREN, a Bitcoin miner that has expanded into AI cloud computing, adds a name from the crypto industry itself.

Under the SEC’s innovation exemption, which the agency unveiled in September after the Clarity Act stalled in the Senate, a venue must notify a company before offering a version of its shares tokenized by an unaffiliated third party. If the company objects in writing within 30 days, the venue can’t make that stock available for trading, according to the SEC’s order. The objection applies only to that venue.

OKXICE TSV, a 50-50 joint venture between NYSE owner Intercontinental Exchange and OKX’s U.S. holding company, plans to let approved users trade tokenized U.S. stocks around the clock. Trades would run through permissioned Uniswap v4 pools on OKX’s XLayer blockchain, paired with stablecoins such as USDC.

None of the objecting companies have explained their decisions, but Nasdaq offered one possible rationale when it opted its own shares out of the exemption.

A spokesperson told The Wall Street Journal that Nasdaq doesn’t want its stock trading on a venue that doesn’t show the best prices in real time or report trades to the consolidated tape.

Further, Crypto in America reported that Nasdaq may be playing a role behind the scenes in encouraging companies to opt out of the OKX-ICE venture. “Our Nasdaq rep had reached out to us regarding OKX listing, suggesting we should object and that they would hold a call to discuss why,” an executive at a Nasdaq-listed company told the publication. “Nasdaq is definitely partly behind this,” the exec reportedly said.

OKXICE’s notice acknowledges it isn’t subject to Regulation NMS, the rules designed to ensure investors get the best displayed prices. It also warns that broker-dealers face unsettled questions about trade reporting and best execution on the venue.

Some of the market’s biggest names, including Apple, Tesla and Amazon, still haven’t appeared on the list.

#5051. THORChain founder rejects freeze request, CZ thanks Secret Service: Hodler’s Digest

Binance has reopened withdrawals following the world’s biggest ever hack, and THORChain co-founder argues it should not halt the protocol to comply with FBI demands.

Binance has reopened withdrawals for 15 blockchain networks, after $6.3 billion was stolen by North Korean hackers from its hot wallets late last week.

Binance said today it had processed around $4.65 billion in withdrawals after reopening and received $1 billion in deposits. Founder Changpeng Zhao said that in the first 12 hours there had only been a 2.1% net outflow and that “98% choose to stay!”

DeFi Llama’s Binance wallet tracker shows $5.8 billion in outflows over 24 hours, however its figures may include internal transfers or movements into new hot wallets.

About 95% of the stolen crypto has already been replenished, using a mixture of Binance’s corporate reserves, insurance and 50% of the funds in the Secure Asset Fund for Users (SAFU).

The biggest hack in history happened on Thursday, when North Korea’s TraderTraitor group gained access to delegated wallet permissions via a compromised laptop. Blockaid CEO Ido Ben-Natan told Cointelegraph that attackers could have stolen the funds without using EIP-7702 (account abstraction), but it may have helped them bypass safeguards and drain assets in one sweep.

Binance co-CEO Richard Teng rejected the idea the hack could be attributed to a single compromised laptop.

“To be clear: the attacker did not simply use one engineer’s permissions, and this was not a single ‘one-click’ action,” he said. “Our investigation has not attributed responsibility to any individual.”

Zhao thanked the Secret Service today for “acting within minutes to help track and freeze stolen funds... your speed, expertise and dedication helped contain the damage.”

Elliptic estimated that about $2.84 billion of the stolen assets, mostly USDC and USDT, had been frozen on chain leaving the hackers with $3.33 billion. The attacker raced against time to beat the unprecedented stablecoin blocks by borrowing about $44.6 million of ETH against stablecoins on Aave.

The FBI attributed blame for the attack to the TraderTraitor hackers, which is associated with North Korea’s Lazarus Group and were also blamed for multibillion hacks of Bybit and Bitget. President Donald Trump indicated “we’ll talk to North Korea about it.” The FBI also released a list of addresses connected to the hack and pointedly singled out THORnode operators, RPC node operators, instant exchanges, DeFi services, blockchain analytics firms and other digital asset service providers to block transactions with or derived from addresses.

The North Korean hackers have used THORChain to permissionlessly swap cryptocurrencies following previous attacks, and the price of the protocol’s native token RUNE jumped 13% in expectation of all those fees. Unfortunately for TraderTraitor, nine chains on THORChain, including Bitcoin, Ethereum and Tron, are currently halted after automated systems paused the protocol for unrelated reasons on October 31.

According to THORChain developer Chad Barraford on the Unchained podcast, only around 50 or so individuals operate the 100 THORChain nodes. If the node operators refuse to halt the protocol or block addresses, as seems likely based on past incidents and public statements, there is speculation they could face prosecution given the scale of the thefts and the involvement of the FBI and Secret Service.

THORChain founder JP Thor (who claims he is no longer involved) argued strongly that THORNode operators should not cooperate and should remain true to the ideals of decentralization and permissionlessness. “IMO if THORNodes decided to take the protocol offline next week then the experiment is over,” he said.

If one third plus one of THORnode operators vote in favor they can halt activity. Nodes can also leave the network if they do not wish to process the funds. THORChain can operate with as few as four nodes processing transactions.

Lookonchain reports the attackers have also been using cross chain platform Relay to swap about half of $95.9 million of stolen SOL into Bitcoin.

Crypto related stocks including Coinbase, Circle and Strategy sold off sharply following the hack, and crypto markets took a dive. But Strive CEO Matt Cole commented on Friday that Bitcoin had held up better than expected.

“I do have conviction that over any reasonable time frame, this does not change the investment thesis on Bitcoin,” Cole said. Bitcoin bottomed out around $76.5K and had recovered to 77,745 at the time of writing. In the wake of the attack, Nasdaq-listed firm Next Technology Holding announced its board has approved selling off its 5833 Bitcoin Treasury worth around $450 million.

The attackers have reportedly amassed at least 18,000 Bitcoin and 430,000 Ether from the theft and subsequent swaps, although some tallies of the funds in the addresses identified by the FBI put the tally much higher at 551,612 ETH and 18,270 BTC. There are concerns that if those currencies are sold off quickly, that it would put downward pressure on BTC and ETH prices.

Meanwhile, Senator Cynthia Lummis and The Digital Chamber both used the hack to again push for crypto legislation to help prevent and mitigate the effects of future attacks.

Trezor has urged customers who bought its hardware wallets from former authorized reseller CryptoBilis since March to move their funds after a report of a suspected implant in a Safe 5 device.

Trezor said Friday that a customer in Indonesia reported an unauthorized circuit board inside a Trezor Safe 5 bought on Tokopedia in August. Trezor said it had not yet examined the device and had received no reports of related losses.

The warning follows reports of crypto losses involving Ledger wallets sold through CryptoBilis that surfaced on Oct. 9. Ledger confirmed an unauthorized hardware implant in one device.

At the end of the week, Bitcoin (BTC) is down 3% to trade at $77,745, Ethereum (ETH) is down 3.5% to trade at $2,260 and XRP (XRP) is down 3.9% to $1.02. The total market cap is at $2.21 trillion according to CoinMarketCap.

Among the biggest 100 cryptocurrencies, the top three altcoin winners of the week are Hyperliquid (HYPE) with a 13.7% gain, Midnight (NIGHT) on 12.6%, and Pepe (PEPE) on 12.6%.

The top three altcoin losers of the week are Curve DAO (CRV) which was down 18.6%, VeChain (VET) down 15.9% and Worldcoin down 13.8%.

Standard Chartered’s Geoffrey Kendrick said one big winner from the hack was likely to be decentralized exchanges and reaffirmed his conviction in Uniswap and the UNI token.

“The decentralized exchange [Uniswap] has never had any exploits, and the hack of the largest centralized exchange, in Binance, should see the ratio of DEX to CEX trading continue to increase.”The ratio is currently around 23.5%. Kendrick said he retains his $100 price target for Uniswap (UNI) for 2030. He said the Binance hack is “another reason why that forecast may end up being too low.”

CFTC chair Mike Selig says autonomous AI agents could challenge financial rules built around human traders, and regulators need to adapt. Selig said Super Intelligence (SI) could give smaller traders and businesses access to risk-management that was once reserved for larger institutions.

“SI can help process the vast amount of information significantly faster than human brain power and with greater accuracy,” he said.

But greater autonomy also comes with new risks. Agents could react unexpectedly to market conditions, while those using similar models could generate correlated trading behavior. The speed of autonomous transactions could also complicate market surveillance and risk management, he said.

Blockchain analytics company Chainalysis said it was targeted in a cybersecurity incident, similar to those suffered recently by other platforms.

“Based on the investigation to date, we have found no evidence of unauthorized access to investigation content, authentication credentials, or session tokens,” it said in a blog post on Friday. Some non-sensitive customer data was exposed, including user IDs, names and email addresses.

Chainalysis said it was using large language models to review its security and added the incident was not related to the recent crypto theft at Binance.

Raymond Christian Garcia and Isiah Angelo Garcia, both of Waller, Texas, were sentenced to 180 months and 186 months in prison, respectively, for their armed robbery of cryptocurrency and kidnapping of a family in Grant, Minnesota.

The brothers were sentenced on Thursday before US District Judge Donovan W. Frank, according to a Friday news release. They had pleaded guilty in June.

On Sept. 19, 2025, the brothers, who had traveled from Texas to Minnesota, kidnapped Victim 1 and his family in their home at gunpoint, court documents said. They zip‑tied the family at gunpoint and held them for more than nine hours, demanding access to Victim 1’s cryptocurrency accounts.

In typical Bitcoin style, the number one cryptocurrency turns 18 amid AI-driven security threats, quantum fears and the biggest exchange hack in crypto history.

Attackers raced to convert stolen stablecoins into crypto that issuers could not freeze as industry players like Tether and Circle moved to immobilize billions of dollars in assets.

A US fusion startup says it has turned mercury into gold. Could a potential increase in the gold supply strengthen Bitcoin’s claim to be a better form of digital gold?

#5049. The 8 Best Natural Deodorants for Men in 2026, Tested by Grooming Editors

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

Sticks that are gentler on your skin and the environment.

SUMMER HEAT MAY be gone, but you still need deodorant. While soaps and body wash rinse away sweat and grime, deodorant keeps odor at bay throughout the day by masking funky smells and killing odor-causing bacteria. So, it’s a crucial part of the defense—no matter the season. If you have sensitive skin or prefer eco-conscious products, natural deodorant is your best bet.

Traditional deodorants use ingredients that may irritate skin or disrupt your body’s natural sweat response, like parabens, phthalates, or, most notably, aluminum. While the FDA does list aluminum as a safe ingredient to be used in deodorants or antiperspirants, to be clear, those with sensitive skin can still find it irritating.

This is because the ingredient can cause inflammation leading to itchiness and rash, as well as worsening dry skin conditions like eczema, says dermatologist Dr. Anna Chacon, MD, FAAD. Not to mention, aluminum can inhibit the body’s natural sweat response by actively plugging pores with heavy metals that stop sweat from being excreted, she says.

Still, there is also a lot of noise out there regarding aluminum contributing to Alzheimer’s disease or cancer, but none have been substantiated. Still, natural alternatives skip this risk and instead rely on plant-based ingredients like essential oils, probiotics, and starches to combat odor and moisture.

Quick reminder: Deodorants do not block sweat; they mask body odor. Only an antiperspirant will do this, but there are no natural antiperspirants, as aluminum is required to plug sweat glands. So, natural deodorants are used as an alternative to both. Plus, they tend to be better for the environment by often using biodegradable ingredients or eco-friendly packaging to reduce waste and plastic pollution.

With all of this in mind, it’s fair to wonder why natural deodorants aren’t the norm. Well, these products have a bit of a bad reputation when it comes to their ability to last as long as traditional aluminum-filled options. While this does hold true for many natural deodorants, there are options on this list that last just as long, and in the case of Ursa Major Hoppin' Fresh Natural Deodorant, which lasts well over 24 hours, outperforms most traditional deodorants.

Salt and Stone incorporates hyaluronic acid and seaweed extract in its natural deodorant to eliminate bad odor, prevent underarm darkening, and deliver intense hydration, which is crucial for preventing funky smells, maintaining health skin, and ensuring proper sweat gland functioning. Not to mention, these ingredients will keep you smelling fresh from morning to night.

While most deodorants (natural and otherwise) have alcohol, which can cause blocked pores, irritation, and dryness, this vegan option leaves it out of the formula. But what really separates Salt and Stone from other brands is its killer natural fragrances.

The santal and vetiver scent smells so good; I’ve often worn it in lieu of cologne. This fresh, subtle sandalwood smell lasts too—all day. The lightweight formula glides on smoothly and soothes my skin. It’s the only natural deodorant I’ve tried that straight up outperforms many of my go-to standard sticks.

It never stains, never aggravates my skin, and rarely needs to be reapplied (only after working out or something especially sweaty). Whether you’re looking for a natural deodorant that smells great or one that’ll keep you protected from odor, Salt and Stone is the best option.

Schmidt’s is a natural deodorant that skips the common bacteria-absorbing and odor-neutralizing baking soda, but still delivers. Packed with skin-friendly ingredients like magnesium and charcoal, it glides on smoothly, dries to a matte finish, and works for virtually all skin types, including sensitive. I have sensitive pits and have faced zero irritation when using this stuff.

The crisp citrus and spearmint scent feels fresh without being overpowering. With just a couple of swipes, you get all-day odor control. I’ll put this on after a pre-work shower and still smell it the next morning when I wake up. Pro tip: Warm the stick briefly against your skin before applying for a smoother glide. While it won’t block sweat, arrowroot and magnesium help absorb moisture and fight funk.

Ursa Major Hoppin' Fresh Natural Deodorant is one of the most reliable natural options out there. It goes on smooth, never stains, and keeps me smelling fresh with its crisp peppermint eucalyptus scent. The formula does use baking soda, so those with sensitive skin may want to look elsewhere. However, it also includes kaolin clay, which absorbs sweat, and hops—just like your favorite beer—to fight odor by neutralizing the growth of odor-causing bacteria (but not sweat, to be clear).

When I use this stick, my skin stays calm and clear. I’ve found that it can ward off odor in the midst of the sweatiest, stickiest summer outings, like a long run or a day at the beach. Plus, I’ve had a Hoppin' Fresh stick last over three months. This thing’s a legit money saver.

Most natural deodorants, understandably, use a lot of fragrance to help mask odor. Somehow, this fragrance-free version neutralizes stink without the aid of added scent. It could be the probiotics in the formula that help to maintain good (non-stinky) bacteria. Or it could be voodoo, who knows? This Hume helped keep B.O. at bay during even the hottest days outside. While it doesn't stop sweat like an antiperspirant, of course, the fragrance-free formula does keep you smelling fresh and clean, somehow. It’s just more like that fresh-out-of-the-shower clean, rather than cologned-up clean.

Some natural deodorants ask you to change your routine—they don’t apply as easily as the traditional ones you’re used to. Native, however, goes on smoothly and doesn’t need to be warmed up or applied with your fingers. This clean vanilla and sandalwood scent is fresh without smelling fake.

The warm, woodsy fragrance is clean and masculine without being overpowering. It goes on cleanly, without any residue, thanks to the twist-up stick. Often natural deodorants, they have a looser texture, but this is the closest to the feel of a traditional stick.

So, if you’re going natural for the first time, it’s the perfect place to start. The included arrowroot and tea tree oil will leave you feeling fresh, and I’ve had one stick last about two months with daily use. This is another one with baking soda, however, so those sensitive might see a reaction (though I didn’t!).

Fighting tough stains can be, well, tough. Enter Malin and Goetz's natural deodorant. With a unique combination of citronellyl and eucalyptus extract, this transparent formula won't leave any traces of residue on your favorite garments. It’s not the strongest deodorant, to be clear, as it will start to fade around the 12-hour mark.

But the cooling scent lasts longer than others that need to be reapplied often. Plus, the lightweight application goes on clear and seemingly evaporates in seconds. I’ve worn black T-shirts at the same time as wearing this deodorant without a trace.

Humble Brands Plastic-Free deodorant is made with just four clean ingredients plus essential oils, and it skips all the junk: no aluminum, parabens, dyes, or synthetic fragrances. The Mountain Lavender scent is earthy and calming, though it may be a bit too strong for those who don’t love an essential oil scent.

What really sold me, however, is the packaging—completely plastic-free, biodegradable, and even the ink is plant-based. Sure, it keeps me dry and odor-free all day, but I also feel good knowing it’s as gentle on my skin as it is on the planet. It’s no wonder Jason Momoa chose Humble to help launch his first deodorant.

Read More: Inside Jason Momoa’s Humble Deodorant

No brand nails masculine, earthy scents quite like Dr. Squatch, and its natural deodorant is no different. The pine tar-scented option, especially, nails the peaceful fragrance of woods at dawn. In fact, it’s the closest I’ve gotten to the indescribable smell of waking up after a night camping in the Sequoia National Forest.

That being said, the deodorant isn’t confirmed to be entirely 100 percent natural. While every ingredient listed could be naturally derived, the brand only confirms 98 percent natural. Still, I never had a reaction with my sensitive skin, and there certainly isn’t any aluminum in the formula. While the application is clean and lightweight, Dr. Squatch also packs one of the longest-lasting scents on this list (up to 48 hours, in my experience).

Again, natural deodorants will never contain aluminum to block sweat. Instead, they use sweat-absorbing ingredients like arrowroot powder, clay, or tapioca starch to manage moisture without stopping sweat entirely. If you see aluminum listed, it’s not truly natural.

Many natural formulas include baking soda to fight odor and absorb wetness, but it can also irritate sensitive skin, causing redness or itching. For those with sensitive skin, baking soda-free options are a safer choice. Natural deodorants also feature soothing moisturizers like coconut oil or other plant-based oils, plus fragrances from essential oils to keep you fresh.

Most natural deodorants mask odor with either synthetic fragrances, like limonene (found in orange peels) and linalool (from plants like lavender and basil), or natural essential oils like lavender, tea tree, or lemon. Neither is inherently better, just comes down to what scent you’re into. But keep in mind that while stronger scents may last longer, there is no guarantee for better odor protection.

Many natural deodorants include extra ingredients to fight odor after the scent fades, including bacterial absorbents like arrowroot, cornstarch, potassium alum, or zinc. If you have sensitive skin, heavy fragrances can still cause irritation, so opt for lighter or unscented formulas.

If you’re going the natural deodorant route for environmental reasons, you’ll probably also want to look for cruelty-free options, which means that the product was not tested on animals. While many grooming products are tested on animals, natural deodorants often prioritize more ethical practices, choosing not to test on animals as part of their commitment to a clean, conscious lifestyle, Dr. Chacon says.

As a grooming editor who was raised on The Grateful Dead not far from Woodstock, I’m intimately familiar with natural, eco-friendly alternatives. For this story, I considered personal favorites and options with high ratings on major e-commerce sites like Amazon. Ultimately, I landed on over 20 natural deodorants to test throughout the past year.

I wore each for at least a week, judging scent, staying power, the way it felt on my skin, and how well they held up through workouts, stress, and overall sweat. From that hands-on testing, these are the eight best natural deodorants for men that actually perform.

The most common argument that natural deodorants are better for you is centered around aluminum. Some claim that aluminum is linked with Alzheimer’s disease, but older studies have been discredited, and there has been no discernible link found between aluminum and Alzheimer’s, according to the Alzheimer’s Association.

It’s also been suggested that aluminum can increase your risk of cancer, particularly breast cancer in women. Dr. Chacon says that more research to determine a concrete link between either is needed, but early studies showing greater aluminum buildup in the brains of Alzheimer’s sufferers is concerning.

There is also usually conversation around preservative parabens, which some claim are linked to cancer as well, and other chemical ingredients that natural deodorants omit from their formulas. While, again, no studies have proven that these ingredients are directly linked to illnesses like cancer, many people like to err on the side of caution.

Apart from all this, traditional deodorants often contain ingredients that can irritate skin, like certain kinds of alcohol or artificial fragrances. Most natural deodorants seek to solve this problem by using naturally derived ingredients that may be more gentle on the skin. The bottom line is that there is no evidence that natural deodorants are better for you than traditional deodorants or antiperspirants; some simply prefer them for their lifestyle and skin.

#5047. Proton Users Revolt After CEO Calls Security Caution 'Loser Thinking' Amid AI Push

Proton Users Revolt After CEO Calls Security Caution 'Loser Thinking' Amid AI Push

Proton users are reporting subscription cancellations after revelations that CEO Andy Yen pressured engineers to embrace AI and dismissed a request for more security testing as “loser thinking.”

The backlash cuts into Proton’s main selling point—trusting it with information people want kept private. Its encrypted email service scrambles messages to protect their contents, and customers questioning its development practices are asking whether faster releases could come at their expense.

A Proton employee asked the company in August for a few days to pentest new code, meaning specialists would try to break into it before release, per 404 Media. Yen replied: “That’s bullshit and loser thinking. Of course it’s possible to be fast and secure.”

He also urged staff to stop writing code by hand and use more AI, and mentioned that he’d be reinterviewing its staff to see if and how they are using AI to do their job, the outlet reported. Its investigation drew on nine current and former employees, internal messages, and meeting recordings.

This didn’t land well among users, and customers have responded with cancellation notices and demands for new leadership on social media such as Reddit. A proposal on Proton’s feedback forum calling for Yen to step down had 2,020 votes when reviewed, alongside comments from users saying they had canceled subscriptions or would not renew.

Besides the blind use of AI, some former and current Proton employees on Reddit and social media have documented a toxic work culture, especially from the CEO.

Those posts document anger, rather than a verified count of departing customers. Canceling a paid plan also does not necessarily mean deleting an account.

Proton’s AI ambitions predate this dispute. Its Lumo chatbot comes with protections against using conversations to train AI, but internal use of AI was not among Proton’s selling points, even in the peak of the chatbot hype.

To address concerns over irresponsible use of AI, on November 1, technology chief Bart Butler published an engineering manifesto arguing that developers should direct large language models, AI systems that generate text and code, rather than treat them as occasional assistants.

Butler compared manual coding to memorizing phone numbers, and envisioned eventually ending most human code review. He also insisted that expertise and testing remain essential, with engineers accountable for work that models cannot reliably handle.

“Your job has fundamentally changed. It is no longer to write code, and LLMs are not here to help you write better code faster,” the manifesto reads. “Your job is to help the LLM write better code faster.” Doubling on its stance, Proton sees avoiding AI as an “existential threat” to the success of its very mission.

“‘Programming’ is dead. Engineering is not,” Buttler told his own team. “Your old job is gone”.

The document also calls for company-wide investment in automation, arguing that generating code faster achieves little if approvals and other development steps remain slow. Butler described that overhaul as the next phase of adoption.

AI tools can introduce security problems of their own. For example, Microsoft researchers said a now patched Claude flaw could expose secret access credentials through malicious instructions hidden in material the coding assistant reviewed.

They can also help find weaknesses: Google’s PageBreak system searches its applications for exploitable bugs and checks whether attacks actually work.

Proton acknowledged to 404 Media that management had sometimes communicated too bluntly and that employees were anxious. Its public manifesto nevertheless argues that failing to adopt AI quickly threatens the company’s ability to compete.

Yen canceled the re-interviews on October 30. The assessments would have required staff to explain their AI use, with employees fearing their answers could determine whether they kept their jobs.

#5046. 8 Things That Should Always Be in Your Field Bag

Game day is only one part of an athlete’s schedule. These versatile picks from Brooks are made for the rest of your routine.

If you’re a regular at your local soccer league, you probably have your game-day uniform down to a science: cleats, socks, shorts, jersey, done. But you also know that training doesn’t end when you leave the field. There are warmups before practice, conditioning sessions, gym workouts, recovery afterwards, and, of course, the inevitable post-game team hangouts while you’re still wearing half your soccer gear. The right off-field pieces can make all of that a little more comfortable.

Brooks has plenty of options that can slot into those in-between moments—from cushioned running shoes for off-field training to lightweight layers for chilly practices to comfortable sweats for the ride home.

Below are eight of our favorite picks for the training, travel, and downtime surrounding the “beautiful game.”

The Glycerin 23 is arguably Brooks’ most versatile off-pitch training shoe. For early-morning practices and chilly fall training sessions, lightweight layers that you can easily move in become essential. It’s an easy option for walking around after a game or practice, whether you’re heading home, meeting teammates for food, or running a few errands.

Sometimes the best post-practice top is simply one you don’t have to think about. This Graphic Short Sleeve has a relaxed fit and a soft cotton-polyester blend, making it a great choice after training or on a rest day. It’s also versatile enough for gym sessions and other low-key activities when you want something more casual and uncomplicated than your usual performance gear.

For outdoor training days when you need more coverage without piling on heavy layers, the Luxe Long Sleeve offers a truly useful middle ground. Its soft, breathable fabric has odor-resistant properties and UPF 50+ sun protection, while thumbholes help keep the sleeves in place. Wear it on its own during a cool-weather workout or underneath a jacket when temperatures really start to dip.

After spending 90 minutes running around in soccer socks, changing into a fresh pair can feel not only like a luxury, but a necessity. The Reset Crew Socks have a soft cotton blend, plush cushioning, a seamless toe, and arch and midfoot compression for a secure feel. An odor-resistant finish is another welcome feature, whether they’re going straight into your gym bag after practice or carrying you into the rest of your day.

#5045. The 8 Best Slip-On Walking Shoes, According to Our Editors

Looking to get out the door quicker? Don’t let laces slip you up.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

SLIDING INTO A pair of slip-on walking shoes might be the easiest thing you’ll do all day. This footwear was built for speed and convenience, making it an accessory that deserves valuable real estate by your door.

Slip-on walking shoes have a rocky history—older versions had a looser fit, raising the risk of instability and friction, especially with higher mileage. But newer designs use no-tie laces, sock liners, pull tabs, and collapsible heels to create a custom fit that rivals what you’ll get with traditional laces.

These shoes are an obvious choice if you have dexterity issues, but they're also just convenient for everyone looking for an easy pair of walking shoes. Slip-on walking shoes also offer more structure than other laceless shoe designs, which has earned them a fanbase with podiatrists. “They are typically a much better option than flip-flops or slides because they provide more stability and keep the foot properly secured,” says Melissa Lockwood, DPM, a podiatrist at Heartland Foot and Ankle Associates in Bloomington, Illinois.

Nicole Graf, DPM, founder of Lexington Podiatry and associate professor at the University of Kentucky, also calls slip-on walking shoes a “huge step up” from flip-flops and slides.

Slip-on walking shoes aren’t for everyone. The fit can still throw some people off, and some simply prefer the tighter feel that laces deliver. But if you want travel-friendly shoes you can toss on in a second, these are a solid choice. As people who value quality, supportive footwear, Men’s Health editors have put in plenty of miles in slip-on walking shoes. The below check all the boxes.

The On Cloud 6 is an excellent example of how good a slip-on shoe can be. You can get in and out of this sneaker with no issue thanks to its wide opening, but the shoe’s elastic “speed” laces and sockliner create a snug fit once you’re in. (Those elastic laces can be adjusted to find your perfect fit.)

The sneaker uses Zero-Gravity CloudTec, which is On’s signature lightweight foam with hollow pods below for a cushioned, yet stable, feel underfoot. (Just be aware that those pods are prone to trapping small pebbles and debris.) A built-in flexible plate, called a Speedboard, helps push you forward with each step. This shoe also comes in a waterproof option, if you’re into rainy strolls. With a wide range of color options and an unobnoxious design, you can pair these with just about anything.

It’s tough to find a quality shoe under $100 right now, let alone one from a big brand. But the Adidas Cloudfoam Cuxxion Sock Shoes have some colorways under $50, making them an ideal choice for budget shoppers who don’t want to mess with laces.

This shoe uses Adidas’ signature Cloudfoam technology at the midsole for a plush, cushioned feel. Its Adiwear outsole is durable and grippy enough for most needs, too. But the real standout in this shoe is its elastic sock upper. It molds to your foot to create a surprisingly snug feel, even for narrow feet. The elastic sock collar supports that secure feel, although it may be too tight for people with wider ankles. The engineered knit upper is highly breathable, lowering the odds you’ll end your walk with sweaty feet. We appreciate the oversized pull tabs in front and back, making the process of getting these on and off seamless.

Feet come in all sizes, making it tough to find a slip-on shoe that works for everyone—and putting adjustable features at a premium. The Nike Pegasus 42 EasyOn has a specialized EasyOn lacing and bungee system up top to help you get the level of tightness you want, before locking it into place. (The lock and bungee combination takes some practice to get right at first.)

The shoe has a collapsible heel that springs back into place once you’re in, making the process of getting these on seamless. It’s also crafted with the same ReactX foam and Air Zoom unit as the standard—and popular—Nike Pegasus 42 running shoe, delivering an energetic feel under your feet. Convenient pull tabs at the heel sit flush against the shoe when they’re not in use, adding to the shoe’s overall sleekness.

Read More: Best Walking Shoes for Men Over 50

Stability shoes are designed to guide your foot with each step and prevent too much inward rolling as you go. But choosing a stability shoe usually means sacrificing a high level of cushioning, since a shoe that feels too plush may not be overly supportive. Hoka’s Skyward Laceless manages to create a stable, locked-in feel while still delivering plenty of the brand’s signature cushioning underfoot.

The Skyward Laceless has a stretchy upper that gently hugs your foot, creating a sock-like feel. The ankle and instep are equally snug, which can be a good or bad thing, depending on your foot size and width. This shoe has plenty of foam below for a slightly bouncy feel, but it still corrects your stride as you go. The integrated tongue pull tab is a nice touch, making for easier entry.

The Under Armour SlipSpeed Mega is packed with features you won’t find in most slip-on walking shoes, creating a versatile, unique sneaker. For starters, this shoe converts from a sneaker design to a recovery slipper with the help of a collapsible heel. (Under Armour refers to this as going from “speed mode” to “slip mode.”)

The shoe skips elastic laces or a sockliner upper in favor of Under Armour’s specialized BOA fit system, a 12-point dial that you can turn to adjust the width across the midfoot. The wide base creates a stable feel, while lots of foam cushioning underfoot delivers a plushness you’ll appreciate. A heads-up: This shoe is slightly heavier than others at 11.7 ounces. That’s a difference you may start to feel on long walks.

After a grueling workout, it feels borderline pampering to slide into the right recovery shoe. The Kane Footwear Active Recovery Shoe is armed with features that should make it a fixture in your gym bag.

This slip-on has strong arch support and a slight rocker shape to help ease pressure on the bottom of your feet as you walk. Internal nodes in the footbed help to promote blood flow and circulation down below, while the shoe’s surprisingly secure design helps prevent slipping. The Active Recovery Shoe is water-resistant with an airy design that allows you to take this in and out of the gym shower without ending up with sopping feet. Heel tabs in back make it simple to get in and out.

Merrell is known for its rugged, outdoor footwear and hiking shoes. While the Merrell Jungle Moc isn’t exactly a hiking shoe, it’s a solid option when you want good traction, whether you’re navigating rocky ground or a slippery surface. The Jungle Moc has been a bestseller for years, with its simple slip-on design and high level of cushioning.

Dual side stretch panels and an exaggerated heel tab make it simple to get in and out of these shoes. The suede upper is breathable, but water-resistant to keep you dry in nearly all conditions. The Jungle Moc has a cushioned EVA midsole for stability and comfort, too. While these shoes are easy to get on, the curved heel makes it harder to slide out—just something to be aware of.

Clogs admittedly aren’t the right style for everyone—some offer less support than sneakers and may slip around more than a standard shoe. But the sleek Crocs Dylan Clog is a solid option for people who want a clog style without some of the common issues that come with it.

The Dylan Clog has a deep heel cup to help lock your foot in, lowering the risk it’ll move around as you step. It’s also made from one piece of Croslite (Crocs’ signature material) to prevent wear and tear over time. This shoe looks like leather thanks to its subtle pebbled texture, but it’s water-resistant and dries quickly when wet. The only major downside is that it’s not very breathable in front. If you’re prone to sweaty toes, this may not be the shoe for you.

Not all shoes are created equal, but the best slip-on walking shoes have all of the supportive features you’ll find in standard lace-up walking shoes. “You mostly get what you pay for in these,” says Richard H. Graves, DPM, a podiatrist at Sol Foot & Ankle Centers in Long Beach, California. He suggests taking a pass at dirt-cheap slip-on walking shoes. “The cheaper ones are not going to provide enough support in the long run, even if they might feel good at first,” Graves says. “There should be enough structure to support your arch and the midsole should be firm and not too flimsy.”

Many high-quality slip-on walking shoes provide similar comfort to regular sneakers. Look for elements like a breathable upper, pull tabs to make them easy to get on and off, and a roomy toe box. Structured heel panels and extra padding in this area help to keep your foot secure, even without standard laces.

Traction for slip-on walking shoes will vary depending on the type of shoe you choose. Most will offer decent traction—meaning, enough so you don’t feel like you’re slipping on wet or damp pavement. But a more rugged shoe like the Merrell Jungle Moc has a grippier sole to help provide extra traction over rockier ground.

Yes, slip-on walking shoes can provide arch support. If this is an important quality for you, look for features like contoured footbeds and a stable midsole. You’ll also want a shoe that fits well and stays put once it’s on to ensure your arch support is consistent. “Make sure that you're not getting them too loose just to make it easier to slide on and off—then you'll be sacrificing support,” Graves says.

To find the best men’s slip-on walking shoes, MH editors tested the most recent and popular pairs on the market, evaluating each for support, comfort, and traction. Because these shoes are designed for walking, not just comfort, durability was also a key factor. To round things out, we consulted three podiatrists for their input on how to evaluate a slip-on walking shoe, along with the value of this style. Based on their advice and our experience, we chose these slip-on walking shoes.

#5044. North Korea Is Laundering Binance Hack Loot at One New Wallet Every 25 Seconds

North Korea Is Laundering Binance Hack Loot at One New Wallet Every 25 Seconds

North Korean hackers are moving the $6.4 billion they stole from Binance at a blistering clip: roughly one new crypto wallet every 25 seconds.

In under four days, the attacker has spread the funds across about 12,000 wallets on 11 blockchains in more than 40,000 transactions, according to an analysis published Monday by blockchain analytics firm Elliptic.

But stablecoin issuers moved even faster. About $2.85 billion, or 44% of the haul, has been frozen or seized, compared with roughly 3% after North Korea’s $1.5 billion hack of Bybit in February 2025.

Speed mattered because stablecoins made up $3.46 billion, or 54%, of the haul, and their issuers can freeze them. Tether froze the stolen USDT on Ethereum 37 minutes after it reached the attacker’s wallet, and Circle froze all $823 million in stolen USDC before any of it could be swapped, Elliptic found.

Before the freezes landed, the hacker swapped 434 million USDT for ETH on decentralized exchanges such as Uniswap, borrowed 19,762 ETH on lending protocol Aave against another 59.9 million USDT, and traded 234 million USDT for Tron’s TRX token. In all, about $786 million in stablecoins got away.

Attackers began draining Binance’s hot wallets late Thursday. The FBI has since attributed the theft to TraderTraitor, a North Korean state-linked hacking group, and published hundreds of linked addresses.

Binance, which has pledged to cover all losses, reopened withdrawals Sunday and saw about $3.64 billion in net outflows in the first nine and a half hours.

The laundering that followed has been relentless. On Tron, which became the main route, activity peaked at about 1,300 transactions in a single hour.

That pace fueled speculation that AI agents were doing the work. Elliptic disagreed, pointing to fixed timing, set amounts, blind retries and pauses that resemble human working hours. The firm wrote that while the laundering is clearly automated, “nothing we have seen shows an AI agent making decisions.”

About $3.24 billion remains in addresses linked to the theft, mostly ETH and the 18,270 stolen Bitcoin, which haven’t moved since the day of the attack. Elliptic believes those funds are very likely still held by North Korea, while money already being laundered may have passed to Chinese money-laundering networks.

Only a negligible amount appears to have been cashed out. About $16 million has also flowed to addresses holding proceeds of September’s Bitget exploit.

Based on the Bybit playbook, Elliptic expects the attacker to swap its ETH for Bitcoin in small batches and cash out through over-the-counter brokers over a period of months. Further freezes will be harder, the firm said, since ETH and Bitcoin can’t be frozen. The best remaining chances lie with centralized services, where the funds will eventually have to surface.

The Binance theft caps a punishing stretch for crypto security. BTCPay Server urged merchants to switch off their servers amid reports of hot wallet attacks on Monday, and Brazilian exchange Mercado Bitcoin halted withdrawals after a $40 million hack. It’s unclear whether either incident is connected to the Binance attack.

#5043. Binance net outflows surge to $5.8B as withdrawals resume after record hack

The exchange says withdrawals surged after some services resumed but have since slowed, with the outflows representing a fraction of its roughly $171 billion in customer assets.

Binance has recorded $5.8 billion in net outflows over the past 24 hours as customers withdraw funds following the exchange’s record $6.3 billion hack, according to data from DefiLlama.

Binance said it began restoring withdrawals at 6:00 UTC on Sunday. By 3:30 UTC, it had processed $4.6 billion in withdrawals against $1 billion in deposits, resulting in net outflows of $3.6 billion.

Separate data from DefiLlama showed $5.8 billion in net outflows over the preceding 24 hours, as funds continued leaving the exchange after its initial update.

Binance said withdrawals initially surged but have since slowed and are stabilizing. The exchange noted that the outflows represented a small portion of the approximately $171 billion in assets it holds. Cointelegraph reached out to Binance for comment but did not receive an immediate response.

The flows offered an early test of customer confidence following the $6.3 billion hack against Binance, the largest crypto theft on record.

Related: Binance says phase one of withdrawals opens after $6.3B hack

Binance’s latest update at 12:36 UTC shows it enabled withdrawals for tokens across 15 networks, including Bitcoin, Ethereum, BNB Smart Chain and XRP Ledger, adding that it will continue to restore additional assets and networks in phases.

Early data shared by Binance shows that the largest share of withdrawals were in USDt (USDT), followed by USDC (USDC) and Bitcoin (BTC).

On Saturday, Binance said it had replaced 90% of the assets lost in the $6.3 billion hack using funds from its corporate treasury, its Secure Asset Fund for Users and its futures insurance fund.
