#5041. On Wants You to Feel Your Softest Ride Yet

The Cloudsurfer 3 is built to make everyday runs feel smooth and effortless, even as the miles add up.

Just because you run doesn’t necessarily mean that you’re up on running shoe innovation. In recent years, there have been notable improvements in propulsion and efficiency, thanks to additions like carbon-fiber plates, smart sensors, and midsoles that promise high energy return. What’s more important than these buzzy words is: How do the shoes feel?

After all, you can have footwear designed by rocket scientists, but if you’re uncomfortable with every step, your run will be a slog, no matter the distance. You want your shoe to make the ride feel soft, which is why the On Cloudsurfer 3 stands out over so many others.

On running shoes have always been distinguished by their trademark hollow pods— the “clouds” that are specifically engineered to help create a softer underfoot experience and give the brand its unmistakable look. In the Cloudsurfer 3, On’s CloudTec Phase™ allows those pods to compress in sequence, helping absorb impact and create a smoother transition from heel to toe. This isn’t just another update to On’s running shoe line. With breakthrough Swiss engineering working beneath every step, the Cloudsurfer 3 delivers the brand’s softest ride yet.

An option for everyday and recovery runs as well as longer training sessions, the Cloudsurfer 3 combines CloudTec® Phase with On’s brand-new SurrealTM Superfoam. According to the brand’s internal testing, the pairing provides 15.5 percent deeper compression—and therefore more impact absorption—than the superfoam alone. The result is a softer landing that can help daily miles feel easier on the legs, particularly over long distances.

According to the same internal testing, the Cloudsurfer 3 is also predicted to deliver a 9.9 percent improvement in smooth-ride sensation. That figure reflects the way the reimagined CloudTec® Phase orientation enhances the rolling motion of the foot, helping each step flow more naturally into the next from landing to toe-off.

You’re also getting a newly engineered mesh upper in the Cloudsurfer 3, which provides a secure fit that adapts to your foot while still offering breathability and support. That breathability helps keep heat and moisture from building up, reducing the conditions that can lead to chafing, blisters, and hot spots.

Even the cushioning around the collar and tongue has been updated, so you get a cloud-like feel as soon as you slip them on. It doesn’t hurt that the Cloudsurfer 3 looks cool, too.

Together, these innovations give runners what they want from an everyday shoe: a natural feel, reliable performance, and lasting comfort. The result is a smoother ride, whether you’re going long or heading out for a quick training run.

That softer feel is a big deal, considering a 2025 study found that runners who perceived their shoes as more cushioned had a lower risk of running-related injury. When a shoe feels right, you’re less likely to think about every step—or adjust the way you’re moving to compensate for discomfort. That allows you to settle into your natural stride and focus on the run itself.

Enjoyment is a major factor, too. Wearing a shoe like the Cloudsurfer 3, with its focus on cushioning, responsiveness, and comfort, can be key to unlocking your best run yet—and enjoying yourself along the way. When that happens, even a hard run can feel softer, mile after mile.

#5040. THORChain halts swaps after block reorg bug, slowing Binance hackers

THORChain node operators paused swaps involving assets on 10 networks, including Bitcoin, Ethereum, Solana and Tron.

THORChain halted swaps across multiple blockchains on Saturday after an Ethereum block reorganization exposed a refund bug, which may have inadvertently slowed the movement of funds stolen from Binance, according to Chainalysis.

According to THORChain, the incident began at 11:34 UTC on Saturday, when a one-block reorganization on Ethereum removed a 10 ETH deposit that THORChain had already credited. When the swap failed to meet its price limit, THORChain issued a refund against funds the vault had never received. The sender’s actual deposit was also refunded, resulting in an extra 10 ETH payout, which then triggered an automatic pause.

An Ethereum block reorganization, or reorg, happens when the network replaces a recent block with a competing block. The newly discovered flaw lets THORChain refund a payment that Ethereum no longer recognizes as having happened. A malicious actor could potentially exploit that mismatch.

“Now that this is public knowledge, every time there is a block re-org on an external chain, a malicious actor could perform a new transaction with the same nonce and would be paid funds by the THORChain vaults,” the protocol’s team said.

THORChain node operators paused swaps involving assets on 10 networks, including Bitcoin, Ethereum, Solana and Tron, while trading continued on Avalanche, Base and XRP, according to the protocol’s network status page.

“Nodes will decide to re-enable chains as they are deemed safe,” said THORChain.

The trading halt also disrupted attempts by North Korean hackers to launder funds stolen in last week’s $6.3 billion Binance hack, according to Chainalysis.

“THORChain just inadvertently delayed the laundering of a significant amount of funds stolen from Binance,” said Chainalysis on Sunday, explaining the emergency pause is “preventing all users, including DPRK’s money launderers, from using cross-chain swaps.”

“Until the service comes back online, neither the hackers nor anyone else can swap through a majority of THORChain’s bridges,” said Chainalysis.

The disruption came as US authorities pressed crypto infrastructure providers to restrict the movement of stolen funds.

On Saturday, the FBI urged THORChain node operators, DeFi services and other virtual asset providers to block transactions associated with addresses used by TraderTraitor to launder proceeds from the Binance hack.

Related: THORChain’s RUNE jumps 13% after Binance’s record $6.3B hack

North Korean hackers have used THORChain to permissionlessly swap cryptocurrencies following previous attacks.

However, THORChain has continued to defend its role as neutral, permissionless infrastructure amid scrutiny over stolen funds moving through the protocol.

In its third-quarter report, the project said it maintained that position despite pressure to block transactions linked to hacked funds.

THORChain founder John-Paul Thorbjornsen separately defended the protocol’s commitment to permissionless transactions, warning against shutting it down in response to pressure over illicit funds.

“IMO if THORNodes decided to take the protocol offline next week then the experiment is over,” he said.

“The last bastion of unencumbered and neutral settlement is over. There will simply be no other venue and the protocol would have failed.”

#5039. Tom Lee Calls Bottom on AI Stock Selloff, Citing Tight Supply and Crypto Resilience

Tom Lee Calls Bottom on AI Stock Selloff, Citing Tight Supply and Crypto Resilience

Fundstrat co-founder and Bitmine chairman Tom Lee says the AI stock selloff has reached its bottom, arguing that demand remains constrained by supply and that crypto’s response to a multibillion-dollar hack suggests investors have already done much of their selling.

“I think we're at the bottom,” Lee said in a CNBC interview on Squawk Box. His call follows a decline that has spread beyond individual AI companies: large technology stocks also sit inside the funds many people use to invest for retirement. (Disclosure: Tom Lee is one of several investors in Dastan, Decrypt’s parent company.)

Lee acknowledged how difficult it is to identify the point where prices stop falling. “Bottoms are dangerous because you don't know how much more downside you have at the bottom,” he said.

His case rests on a distinction between an industry struggling to expand fast enough and one that has built more capacity than customers need.

“The market for AI is still very tight. It's not an overcapacity market,” Lee said. Constraints on spending, including political opposition, are slowing growth without signaling that the boom has peaked, he argued.

The distinction matters for companies building the infrastructure behind AI, including data centers, the facilities that house the computers running these systems. A shortage can leave customers waiting, but it can also prevent suppliers from turning orders into revenue.

Intel offered a particularly sharp example on Friday, when its shares plunged nearly 30% to $65.76 after a disappointing fourth-quarter forecast. Its data center and AI revenue had risen 78% from a year earlier to $7.3 billion, yet manufacturing constraints limited its ability to meet demand.

Intel projected quarterly revenue of $15 billion to $16 billion, with the midpoint about $1.6 billion below analysts’ expectations. Its weaker PC business added pressure, showing why healthy AI demand can coexist with a falling share price.

Lee’s second argument looks outside the stock market. He pointed to crypto’s relatively limited response to the Binance security breach, which the exchange initially estimated had an impact of about $6.3 billion.

“If crypto is a leading indicator for risk, you've already seen people sort of washed out,” he said. In his reading, a market that absorbs bad news without a much larger decline may have fewer sellers left to push prices down.

Bitcoin did fall about 4% to roughly $76,400 on Friday, and more than $1.3 billion in positions funded with borrowed money were forcibly closed. U.S. spot Bitcoin exchange-traded funds, which hold Bitcoin and trade like stocks, also shed $643.2 million that day, their largest daily outflow of October.

That said, a 4% drop can happen even during bull runs and it is considered a relatively normal occurrence among crypto traders.

Binance said on Friday that about $3 billion of the affected assets had been frozen and pledged to cover losses using its emergency reserve and company funds.

#5037. The Problem With Pain: Suffering and the Myth of Growth

We’re all holding onto something we’re sure we can’t give up on, but research shows that letting go can boost our happiness a lot more than we might think.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 1, 2026 | Reviewed by Jessica Schrader

A friend of ours recently totaled their car in an accident. The resulting concussion was worse. Concussions are never fun, but this one lingered.

It’s often said that pain can help us dig deep, find that extra gear, and become stronger, more resourceful people. Maybe. But pain can also lead us into a maze of suffering, with no clear sense of when, or whether, we’ll find our way out.

With some kinds of pain, we have a reasonable sense of what’s wrong and what recovery will look like. It hurts, but we expect it to pass.

Suffering can threaten our very sense of who we are. The distinction goes beyond the severity of the symptoms: it concerns what the experience means for us as people. Physician Eric Cassell described this threat to the person as central to suffering.

When we’re suffering, it can be hard to believe that what we’re experiencing is temporary. It could go on forever. And even if we come out the other side, will we come out intact? We may feel that something essential is being taken from us. We may question our purpose, see ourselves as burdens to loved ones, or feel that we’ve lost the capacity to care for ourselves or contribute to others. Suffering may begin with a physical injury, but its effects reach much further.

That’s what our friend is dealing with. The symptoms, the uncertainty, the disrupted routines, the inability to participate in work and social life as before.

So how should we support the people we care about who are suffering?

It’s not always clear. But putting pressure on them to be positive can worsen the burden. On top of what they’re already going through, they may feel added guilt for not handling it better. Research by Brock Bastian and colleagues suggests that feeling pressure not to experience negative emotions can make people feel worse. Post-traumatic growth seems like a big ask when someone is just trying to cope.

There’s also reason to be cautious about the claim that suffering makes us stronger. People sometimes report growth after trauma, but that doesn’t necessarily mean they’ve changed in the ways they believe they have. In a prospective study of undergraduates, Patricia Frazier and colleagues found that reported growth generally did not correspond to changes measured before and after a traumatic event. The story we tell afterward may help us make sense of the experience, but that doesn’t mean it describes a measurable change.

And that’s OK. People can cope without growth. In two studies of people exposed to war or terrorism, Stephen Levine and colleagues found that participants with the greatest resilience reported the least post-traumatic growth. Here, the researchers defined resilience as the absence of post-traumatic stress disorder. People don’t need to emerge stronger to have coped well, and the process of coping may not leave much left in the tank.

Instead of asking them to change their perspective, we can start by taking their symptoms and suffering seriously. Listen without trying to supply a silver lining. Support modest, achievable goals that matter to them, while recognizing that their capacity may change from day to day. Help with practical burdens.

Specific help that addresses a concrete need is often easier for them to accept. Take care of an errand. Bring over a casserole. Keep it simple, too; don’t make your friend have to organize your assistance. And remember: they may be in that maze for a while. That means they may still need help long after the initial attention has faded.

When we offer encouragement, it should be concrete and credible. Acknowledge something they’ve managed or a difficulty they’ve faced. We don’t have to make the situation sound cheerful or promise that everything will work out.

We can care about their recovery without requiring them to find a lesson in it. And we can keep showing up while they find their own way through.

#5036. Bank of Korea probes financial firms’ exposure to crypto risks

The central bank is surveying 31 financial institutions on how a major exchange shutdown, prolonged crypto downturn or losses at another firm could affect their operations.

The Bank of Korea has reportedly asked banks and brokerages about cryptocurrency-related risks, asking how they would be affected by a major exchange hack, a market crash or heavy losses at another financial institution.

The central bank began surveying 18 banks and 13 brokerages on Oct. 23 as part of its first reported review of financial institutions’ participation in the virtual asset market, according to The Herald Business and Yonhap, citing financial industry sources.

The survey includes questions about each institution’s crypto-related staffing and budgets, business activities and reasons for entering the market, along with amounts invested in domestic and overseas crypto companies.

One scenario asks how financial firms would be affected if South Korea’s largest crypto exchange by market share temporarily suspended operations following a major hack or similar incident.

Other questions address a steep fall in cryptocurrency prices followed by a sustained trading slump, and the potential fallout if another financial institution suffered heavy losses from a crypto-related incident.

The survey began before last week’s $6.3 billion Binance hack, but the incident highlights the type of risk the central bank is examining. US authorities have attributed the attack to North Korean hackers.

Responses are reportedly due on Wednesday.

The review comes as South Korean financial firms have continued testing stablecoin services.

KB Financial Group completed a won-denominated stablecoin pilot in May, while Kakao Pay and KakaoBank partnered with Fireblocks in September to explore digital asset infrastructure.

The Bank of Korea has pushed for bank-led stablecoin issuance, while disagreements over which institutions should be permitted to issue stablecoins have complicated progress on the proposed Digital Asset Basic Act.

“Financial institutions are making their own preparations even as virtual asset legislation is moving more slowly than the market expected,” a Bank of Korea official told The Korea Herald.

Regulators have also stepped up cybersecurity scrutiny. In October, the Financial Services Commission proposed tougher security requirements after finding deficiencies at 130 of 555 financial companies.

The proposals include more frequent vulnerability assessments and greater executive accountability, following security incidents involving 16 companies, including crypto exchanges.

#5035. Influencers Are Doing ‘Olive Oil Shots.’ We Asked 4 Experts If That’s a Good Idea—and They All Basically Said the Same Thing.

The truth about the supposed health practice—revealed.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

SO OLIVE OIL shots are a thing again. Remember in the mid- to late-2010s, when people were knocking back all manner of liquids, including apple cider vinegar, juiced ginger, pulverized turmeric, and extra virgin olive oil? Well, social media influencers have brought back at least the last one, with Bryan Johnson doing “evo” shots and Steven Gundry, MD, recommending a daily dose of the stuff. (Also, both those guys sell their own olive oil. Just saying.)

The supposed benefits of shooting olive range from the more generalized “more energy” to more specific ones, like protecting your cardiovascular system from disease.

Influencers usually back up these claims by citing scientific research on the benefits of adding olive oil to your daily diet. But do the benefits extend to doing shots of olive oil?

“Olive oil is great for heart health and contains beneficial polyphenols, but you don't need to take it as a shot. You get the same benefits by just including it in your meals, and it's generally much more enjoyable that way. But if you want to take it as a shot, there's no harm in that. It's just not superior to including olive oil in your diet in regular ways. You know, with food.”

“I'm a fan of California Olive Ranch 100% California Extra Virgin Olive Oil For Everyday. It's high quality, reasonably affordable (for evoo), and widely available. I use it to saute veggies, roast tubers and veggies, and drizzle on various things.”

“I'm a bigger fan of eating a few shots of olive oil with sauteed veggies or I pour it on my food. But a shot is fine I guess. People like to do it in the longevity scene to invoke ancient Greeks doing shots of ‘liquid gold.’”

“I am an affiliate of the Fresh Pressed Olive Oil Club, which I reallly love. I've not had lots of fancy, fresh olive oils from around the world before and now I have. I like seeing and tasting the diversity. I use a 1-liter glass bottle of EVOO, usually Braggs or Whole Food Organic. I try to go through two to three of these a month for our house of four.”

“I wouldn’t suggest shots of olive oil for any reason. All that’s happening here is that you’re adding a concentrated source of calories and fat to your diet. There are no documented benefits of doing this for the average person, and it can cause weight gain if the oil shots are taken in addition to someone’s current diet. This can ultimately cause health problems.”

“I don’t really have a fave oil. I use brands that have a seal of origin in them to ensure their purity.”

“While olive oil is full of beneficial compounds that may support heart health and longevity, shooting it isn't necessary. Heating olive oil may degrade some beneficial polyphenols, but using it as a salad dressing, sauce, or finishing oil for roasted veggies provides the same benefits, plus enhanced absorption of fat-soluble vitamins and carotenoids.”

“Extra-virgin olive oil can have such a range of flavors. I love San Giuliano Coastal Harvest Olive Oil. It's cold-pressed, which helps preserve more nutrients and polyphenols, and has a really nice, peppery flavor. While I don't take shots of it, I regularly use it in dressings and over veggies.”

He is also the author of two Men's Health cookbooks, Guy Gourmet and A Man, A Pan, A Plan, and the winner of a James Beard Award.

#5034. BTCPay Server warns of hot wallet thefts running on outdated software

BTCPay Server urged operators to shut down their servers after receiving reports of unauthorized attackers draining funds from hot wallets.

Update (Nov. 3, 2026, 7:00 am UTC): This article has been updated to include comments from a BTCPay Server spokesperson and additional information from the project’s official security advisory.

Attackers have stolen Bitcoin and Litecoin from users running outdated BTCPay Server software, exploiting a security flaw the project fixed in August.

The Bitcoin payment platform said Monday that the thefts affected versions 2.4.1 and earlier. Users who installed the Aug. 7 fix before Sunday appeared unaffected, the project said.

BTCPay initially urged operators to shut down their servers while it investigated the unauthorized wallet withdrawals. The project also asked hosting providers to take BTCPay Server instances offline as a precaution.

The vulnerability was also involved in an August attack that exposed credentials for Lightning payments. BTCPay said its onchain hot wallets were unaffected then, but attackers have since used a different method to exploit the flaw.

The latest thefts affected hot wallets, which keep the keys needed to spend funds on the server. Funds in wallets that keep those keys elsewhere, including hardware wallets, were unaffected.

BTCPay urged users still running vulnerable software, or who updated after the warning, to rebuild their servers and move funds to newly created wallets. It gave the same advice to anyone who found evidence of a break-in.

BTCPay has not disclosed how many users reported thefts or how much was stolen. “BTCPay is a self hosted project, every user deploys their own server, we as a free and open source software have no way to track users,” a spokesperson told Cointelegraph.

#5033. Claude-Built Linux Exploit Targets Bug That Ubuntu Still Hasn't Patched

Claude-Built Linux Exploit Targets Bug That Ubuntu Still Hasn't Patched

Another day, another exploit from ChatGPT or Claude that gives us the chills.

Anthropic says its AI wrote a working attack that lets a stranger take full control of a Linux server over the internet, no password needed. The bug behind it, CVE-2026-98432, has been hiding in Linux since version 4.4 and scores 9.8 out of 10 for severity.

Linux runs countless servers, and Ubuntu is one of its most popular flavors. Ubuntu's own tracker still lists every version from 16.04 to 26.10 as vulnerable.

Anthropic described the attack on Oct. 27 in a research post covering five targets: Chrome, Safari, Android, Windows, and Linux. Claude wrote the Linux exploit after initial prompting, using an unreleased internal model.

The attack takes less than an hour to find a hidden spot in the computer's memory, then five more minutes to run a command with the highest level of access, known as root.

The flaw works like reading a sticky note after someone already shredded it. One part of the system throws away a piece of information while another part is still using it, per AlmaLinux. A specially crafted message that opens an internet connection can set that off.

Failing costs the attacker almost nothing. "An enabling property of this race condition is that a lost race only fails the handshake without crashing the whole system," Anthropic wrote, "so a determined attacker can retry indefinitely until they succeed."

Anthropic's version needs a server using a feature called multipath TCP, which lets one connection travel over several routes at once. Programs written in Go have switched it on by default since Go 1.24, per the Go Security Team.

But the bug is not limited to that feature. "While the demonstrated exploit requires the target system to expose an MPTCP listener, the underlying bug is not specific to MPTCP," the Go Security Team wrote.

Red Hat released fixes for RHEL 8, 9 and 10 on Sunday, including RHSA-2026:93412. Debian, SUSE, Oracle Linux and Amazon Linux have also shipped fixes, at least for some versions.

For anyone who can't update yet, Red Hat published a temporary firewall rule. It "reduces exposure to this vulnerability but cannot fully prevent it," Red Hat says in its security bulletin. A full working solution is still under development.

Anthropic's earlier Claude Mythos model helped Mozilla find 271 bugs in Firefox, as Decrypt reported in April.

Ubuntu's tracker shows fixes in progress for 22.04, 24.04, 26.04 and 26.10. Versions 20.04, 18.04 and 16.04 show only "Vulnerable," and the tracker gives no release date for any fix.

#5031. Bitcoin ETFs sink into October outflows with $643M exit after Binance hack

US spot Bitcoin ETFs ended October with $515 million in net withdrawals, while Ether funds posted a record $1.61 billion in monthly outflows.

Investors pulled $643 million from US-listed spot Bitcoin exchange-traded funds (ETFs) on Friday, pushing October’s net flows into negative territory as crypto markets faced volatility following Binance’s $6.3 billion hack.

Friday’s withdrawals snapped four consecutive days of inflows, leaving the funds with $515 million in net outflows for October, their first negative month since June, according to SoSoValue. The funds still attracted approximately $361 million in net inflows for the year through October.

BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) led Friday’s outflows with $234 million, followed by Fidelity’s Wise Origin Bitcoin Fund (FBTC) with $201 million, according to Farside Investors data.

The withdrawals followed Bitcoin’s drop to $77,000 on Thursday. Binance, the world’s largest crypto exchange by trading volume, confirmed Friday that hackers had stolen about $6.3 billion in crypto assets.

Hardware wallet maker OneKey’s founder and CEO, Yishi, warned on X Friday that IBIT could face similar security threats because it relies heavily on Coinbase to hold its Bitcoin.

Investors also withdrew a net $156 million from US spot Ether (ETH) ETFs on Friday, bringing October’s net outflows to a record $1.61 billion. The previous monthly high was $1.42 billion in November 2025.

October’s withdrawals also erased the Ether funds’ net inflows for 2026, leaving them with roughly $45 million in net outflows for the year.

Clothing designed for movement but stylish enough for everyday wear. Think workout shirts, joggers, sweat-wicking polos, gym shorts, and sneakers that work for both gym sessions and casual outings. Brands like Alo and Patagonia offer a solid collection of these duds.

Easygoing staples like jeans, hoodies, henleys, sneakers, and flannels—perfect for relaxed weekends, brunch, or laid-back office days. Brands such as Abercrombie, J.Crew, Everlane, and Uniqlo deliver in this category.

When shopping for the best clothing brands for men, fit is everything. It dictates not only how comfortable you feel but also how your outfit communicates your personal style. Generally, men’s garments are broken down into four broad categories: Relaxed, Tailored, Classic, and Slim. You don’t have to stick to just one, but each serves a different aesthetic and function—let’s break it down.

Relaxed-fit clothing is all about comfort. It’s cut with extra room through the chest, waist, and legs, allowing for a looser, more laid-back silhouette. This is ideal if you’re looking for casual wear, streetwear, and workwear-inspired looks. Think boxy tees, oversized hoodies, wide-leg trousers, and unstructured outerwear. Patagonia tends to lean into this fit as its fleece jackets, outerwear, and casual basics are built for movement and outdoor adventure. Alex Mill focuses on casual staples with slightly relaxed, vintage-inspired cuts.

A tailored fit offers a balance between structure and comfort. It follows the body’s shape without clinging too tightly, creating a refined yet wearable look. This is your go-to fit for business casual, smart casual, and elevated everyday wear. Think tapered chinos, slightly structured blazers, and slim-but-not-skinny button-downs.

Slim fits are designed for a clean, modern silhouette. These clothes contour the body without excessive looseness, making them ideal for dressier looks, contemporary minimalism, or even athleisure. Key pieces include slim-fit jeans, fitted turtlenecks, and sharply cut blazers.

Classic fit is the middle ground between relaxed and tailored, giving you room to move without excess fabric. This is the fit you’ll find in traditional menswear, from business suits to heritage denim. It’s not baggy, but it’s also not hugging your body either.

Not all clothes are created equal, and neither are price tags. Some items are worth dropping some serious cash on, while others can be a rip-off. The key is knowing when to invest and when to save because a well-thought-out wardrobe isn’t about spending the most, it’s about getting the most value out of what you buy.

This is your fast-fashion and essentials tier—solid for trying new styles or grabbing wardrobe basics without overthinking. But here’s the catch: Cheap can often mean short-lived. For instance, a $10 tee from a fast-fashion retailer may look good today but will likely warp, fade, or pill after a few washes. That’s fine for trends like an oversized striped tee you might wear for a season, but when it comes to daily staples, spending a little more pays off. You’d be better off spending $58 on an Alex Mill slub tee that lasts for years and is worn 50-plus times, which equals less than $2 per wear.

This is where cost meets quality. You’re getting better fabrics, stronger construction, and brands that care about longevity. Think nicer denim, well-cut chinos, and quality knitwear—things you’ll actually wear often, so the investment makes sense.

This is where justifiable spending comes into play. A $500 Buck Mason suede jacket? Pricey up front, but wear it 50 times per season for five years, and suddenly, it’s $2 per wear—less than a daily coffee. The same goes for boots—drop $300+ on a well-made pair, and you won’t have to replace them for a decade. In this price range, you find premium outerwear, leather goods, boots, and everyday pieces.

At this level, you’re paying for craftsmanship, exclusivity, and long-term investment. A $1,300 leather jacket from Todd Snyder isn’t for everyone, but if you’re buying into history, durability, and premium construction, it’ll outlive you. Same with a properly tailored suit—if you wear it for major life events over decades, it’s an investment, not an expense.

To find the best men's clothing brands, our fashion and commerce teams, led by Style and Reviews Director Christian Gollayan, spent the last five years putting over 80 menswear brands through the wringer, wearing threads to sweaty gym sessions, breezy hikes, long workdays, formal cocktail events, and casual weekend outings. Whether it was testing a fleece in the cold, seeing if dress shirts hold up in marathon meetings, or figuring out if chinos had enough stretch for comfort during a long-haul flight, we made sure these brands actually delivered on function as well as style.

From there, we narrowed it down to 16 standout brands based on quality, fit, signature styles, type of garment, and value. No fast fashion, no fleeting trends, just well-made, great-fitting essentials that look sharp on any guy, hold up over time, and feel like a solid investment for our wardrobe. #5029. OKX draws $445M in net inflows as Binance outflows exceed $7B

OKX led cryptocurrency exchanges with $445 million in daily inflows, while Binance processed about $7 billion in net outflows.

OKX recorded about $445 million in 24-hour net inflows, the largest among tracked crypto exchanges, while Binance continued to register outflows after reopening withdrawals following its $6.3 billion hack.

Crypto exchange Bybit was in second place with $112 million in net inflows, followed by Gate with $91 million and Bitget with $54 million in 24-hour net inflows, according to DefiLlama.

Binance recorded the biggest net outflows among tracked exchanges, with more than $7 billion, up from $5.8 billion earlier Monday. Binance said it began restoring withdrawals in phases at 6:00 a.m. UTC on Sunday.

At the time of writing, Binance held about $127.9 billion in wallets tracked by DefiLlama, more than four times the $29 billion at OKX, the second-largest exchange by that measure.

Binance suspended withdrawals Friday, soon after late Thursday’s $6.3 billion hack, the largest in cryptocurrency history.

By Sunday, Binance said it had replenished approximately 95% of the affected assets, up from 90% on Saturday. The remaining 5% consisted of less liquid tokens that it continued to buy through market and over-the-counter transactions.

The exchange is using its corporate treasury, a partial deployment of its Secure Asset Fund for Users (SAFU) and a portion of its futures insurance fund to replace the stolen assets. Binance has pledged to absorb the losses without passing them on to customers.

About $3 billion in stolen assets frozen by industry partners is separate from Binance’s replacement funds. Binance said Sunday it published a new reserves snapshot and an updated proof-of-reserves report was expected Monday.

OKX’s OKB token rose about 1.4% over the past 24 hours to trade near $113 on Monday, while BNB gained 0.3% to about $708 over the same period, according to CoinMarketCap.

#5028. Is the Documentary "You Can See Everything" Inaccurate?

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Updated November 1, 2026 | Reviewed by Jessica Schrader

The A24 (meta-) documentary You Can See Everything about Elizabeth Holmes, founder of the now-defunct health technology company Theranos, got the internet abuzz. If you watch it, you will see why. The feature is both thought-provoking and unusual, and the main subjects featured in it—Holmes and her partner Billy Evans—have the screen presence of highly-paid actors playing Holmes and Evans.

As one might expect amid so much attention, there are criticisms as well. A key objection made is that the film is inaccurate. One can see why some people think that. There is, for instance, a scene in the film in which Holmes and one of the directors, Nathan Fielder, debate the meaning of the word “can” in the expression “can run every test.” Holmes insists that the device she is showing to Fielder “can run every test.” Fielder suggests that Holmes is not using the word “can” in the usual way. (Note: In one sense of “can,” I can defeat the world chess champion at chess, namely, it is not physically impossible for me to move the pieces on the board so as to score a victory. But if I say I can defeat the world chess champion, you will take me to be saying something different and altogether false. It may also be that Holmes’ device can run every test in some such sense. That, of course, is not the sense that would have interested either customers or investors.) In another scene, Holmes is shown Abbott and Costello’s “Who’s on First?” comedy sketch, and she seems to either not get or pretend not to get the joke, which involves understanding that “Who,” “What," and “I Don’t Know” are names of baseball players.

These and other scenes may leave viewers with the impression that Holmes simply doesn’t use words in the way most people do, and that perhaps, she is not so much a fraudster as a highly idiosyncratic language user with less-than-stellar communication skills.

Were that the message, the film would be, indeed, inaccurate. Holmes is not in prison because of miscommunication. She falsified financial records, diluted people’s blood with water and had the samples analyzed by real labs then claimed to have used the Theranos device, and she did other similar things.

However, I am not inclined to view the film as misleading, because I don’t know what, if anything, it is asserting. A conventional documentary asserts—i.e., invites audiences to believe—various claims about the characters and events it depicts, and if the claims asserted are false, the documentary is, to that extent, inaccurate. But You Can See Everything is not a conventional documentary. For one thing, Fielder mentions at the very beginning that he is not “good at science things” and asks Holmes whether that was why she invited him and not someone else better equipped to ask questions about the technology. This can be taken as a disclaimer on his part: Viewer beware.

No less importantly, Fielder and Oppenheim could have spoken to other people better-versed in both the science and in the details of the case. This is what one might get in a conventional documentary. Fielder and Oppenheim do not do that either, and the choice seems deliberate. That too may be taken as a signal to the viewer that the filmmakers did not intend to explain “what really happened.”

One can argue, perhaps, that in engaging with Holmes in a discussion about the meaning of “can,” Fielder was deliberately introducing ambiguity to make the story more interesting. But I am not sure that’s true either. An alternative interpretation is this: Fielder wanted to see how far he could get with Holmes if he played on her turf and started from her own premises. Suppose she really did use the word “can” in an atypical way. Shouldn’t she have nonetheless anticipated that this would lead to a misunderstanding? As for the Abbott and Costello sketch, that may have been bait to see what Holmes would say about it. She can be seen laughing while watching it. That suggests she understood what’s funny. What would she say if asked? She denies she understood. The documentary does not assert that her denial is in good faith. An alternative interpretation is that we see Holmes seizing the opportunity and trying to pull the veil over our eyes while falling for Fielder’s trick.

I, for one, am very glad that Fielder and Oppenheim took the approach they took. The actual story has been covered previously, and more than once. The world doesn’t need another run-of-the-mill Holmes documentary, however accurate. What You Can See Everything offers, instead, is an attempt to penetrate what looks like Holmes’ façade. It does not succeed despite the fact that the team has full access for an extended period of time. It is a bit as though the lodgers with their cameras keep knocking on the door even though they are already inside the house. They never get behind the immaterial partition which separates them from the thoughts of their subjects. Just why that is is one of the themes of the film.

In a widely viewed trailer, Holmes is heard saying to Fielder, “I am engaging with you as a human being” and “I am always being real.” It is really the filmmakers who seem to want to engage with Holmes as a human being, but they only get her to reflect their own intentions back at them. Since their main goal was to create an entertaining feature and they succeeded at that, perhaps, we can hand them the victory. But Holmes’ own play remains a mystery, so one could say they succeed on Homes' terms. In the game of manipulation, Holmes and the film-makers draw.

The feature is unconventional—though, importantly, not in a way that’s parasitic on prior conventions, as when an “invisible” artwork seeks to thwart the expectation that artworks in general have some material bearer. The movie feels genuinely original. It surprises viewers without trying to trick them by leading them to expect one thing and giving them another. The work is exploratory, too. It doesn’t promise to deliver anything in particular—least of all, “the truth about Holmes”—but it delivers.

Precisely what it is that it delivers is not easy to say, but that’s part of its point—and its charm.

I would not call the film inaccurate. Of course, it isn’t accurate either. It is something else.

#5026. Mystery $106 Million Crypto Theft Hits EU Entity Days After Binance Hack

Mystery $106 Million Crypto Theft Hits EU Entity Days After Binance Hack

Another nine-figure theft has hit crypto, just days after the biggest heist in the industry’s history.

An unnamed entity in the European Union is suspected of losing more than $106 million from several of its accounts on centralized exchanges on Sunday, pseudonymous onchain investigator ZachXBT said Monday in a post to his Telegram channel.

The funds were withdrawn as USDC and USDT stablecoins by what he called an “illicit actor.”

ZachXBT published 11 Ethereum addresses tied to the theft.

Instant exchanges let users quickly swap one cryptocurrency for another, often with little identity verification, while Monero hides senders, receivers and amounts, making it a popular tool for obscuring stolen funds.

Swapping out of USDC and USDT also matters because the companies behind those stablecoins can freeze them.

ZachXBT said the culprit remains unidentified, but that he could say “with a high degree of confidence” that it isn’t Lazarus Group, citing multiple laundering indicators. Lazarus is the umbrella name often used for North Korea’s state-backed hackers. ZachXBT didn’t name the victim.

That sets the theft apart from the record-setting $6.4 billion Binance hack, which the FBI has attributed to TraderTraitor, a North Korean hacking group.

In that case, stablecoin issuers moved within minutes. Tether froze stolen USDT on Ethereum in 37 minutes, and Circle froze all $823 million in stolen USDC, helping lock up about 44% of the haul, according to blockchain analytics firm Elliptic.

North Korea’s launderers have since spread the remaining funds across about 12,000 wallets. It’s unclear whether any funds in the EU case have been frozen.

The incident adds to an already unprecedented week for crypto security. Beyond the record-breaking Binance hack, Brazilian exchange Mercado Bitcoin just today reported halting withdrawals after a $40 million hack of its own. Likewise today, BTCPay Server told merchants to switch off their servers amid reports of hot wallet attacks.

#5025. BTC heads into US midterm showdown: Three things to know in Bitcoin this week

Bitcoin price action held its 100-day moving average into the monthly close as crypto markets braced for US midterm election and tech earnings volatility.

Bitcoin (BTC) held above its 100-day moving average on Monday ahead of US midterm elections, jobs data and earnings from Apple and Palantir.

Tuesday’s US midterm elections will determine control of Congress, with prediction market traders heavily favoring a Democratic victory in the House of Representatives.

Kalshi put the probability of Democrats winning the House at 93.6% as of Monday.

President Donald Trump said he would use his veto power if Democrats took control of Congress.

“I guess I’ll become Mr Veto. They’ll be sending stuff up. You veto it,” he said, according to the New York Post.

US midterm elections odds (screenshot). Source: Kalshi

Bitcoin ended the fourth quarter lower in each of the past three US midterm election years, falling 17.3% in 2014, 43.5% in 2018 and 14.8% in 2022. All three periods coincided with Bitcoin bear markets.

Benjamin Cowen, founder and CEO of crypto analytics platform IntoTheCryptoverse, suggested that the midterms can function as a cue to increase BTC exposure due to their short-term bearish impact on price.

“Usually the Q4 dump of midterm years just provide opportunities to buy for those who sold long ago,” he said Friday on X.

In July, Cowen referenced Bitcoin bear-market bottoms coinciding with the second half of midterm years.

BTC/USD was down 6.4% for the quarter as of Monday, after its weakest October since 2014, according to CoinGlass.

BTC/USD liquidation heatmap. Source: CoinGlass

September US job openings data are due Tuesday, followed by October’s nonfarm payrolls report on Friday.

Payrolls are expected to rise by 65,000, after the economy added 29,000 jobs in September, below forecasts for 84,000. Such an outcome notionally increases the odds of dovish policy signals from the Federal Reserve, which is in turn bullish for crypto and risk-asset liquidity.

Cleveland Fed President Beth Hammack and Fed Governor Michelle Bowman are among the officials due to speak this week.

Data from CME Group’s FedWatch Tool currently shows that markets expect the Fed to raise interest rates by 0.25 percentage points at the December meeting of the Federal Open Market Committee (FOMC). The odds of such an outcome have sat between 67% and 75% for the past month.

Fed target rate probability comparison for December FOMC meeting (screenshot). Source: CME Group

The University of Michigan will also release its preliminary November consumer sentiment survey on Friday, including consumers’ inflation expectations.

Alongside the macro data prints, 20% of S&P 500 companies are due to report earnings in the coming days. Chief among these are tech giants Apple and Palantir, both due to report after the market close on Monday.

The reports follow last week’s tech sell-off, when disappointing guidance renewed concerns about AI spending and profitability.

Economist Mohamed El-Erian described markets as now “acutely sensitive” to tech-sector news headlines in weekend analysis.

“The market is particularly focused on justification for the massive capital expenditures being deployed across the sector, ROI’s, and revenue growth,” he wrote.

After closing October down 7.3%, Bitcoin is holding above a moving average that has provided support since mid-August.

Related: Here’s what happened in crypto today

TradingView data showed BTC/USD staying above last week’s low near $76,300, without retesting it around the monthly close.

That low was close to the 100-day simple moving average (SMA) for BTC/USD, which has since risen to $76,548.

Bitcoin also reclaimed its 100-week exponential moving average (EMA) in time for the monthly close, with this standing at $77,759.

BTC/USD daily chart with 100 SMA, 100 EMA. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

Trader and analyst Rekt Capital compared the weekly chart with Bitcoin’s rebound from its 2022 bear market low. During the recovery, BTC/USD produced an inverse head-and-shoulders pattern, retested support at the right shoulder, briefly fell below it and then rallied.

Last week’s drop to $76,300 brought Bitcoin back to what Rekt Capital identified as right-shoulder support on the current chart. He is watching for a brief move below that level as a possible next step in the comparison.

“The question now is whether Bitcoin can produce some sort of downside deviation below here over the next few weeks,” Rekt Capital said.

BTC/USD one-week chart. Source: Rekt Capital on X.com

#5022. Hong Kong takes tokenized settlement to 24/7, plans stablecoin trials

The HKMA plans to bring regulated stablecoins into tokenized government debt trials, while HSBC says its RedCoin stablecoin has reached 50,000 users.

Hong Kong has expanded its Project Ensemble pilot to allow round-the-clock settlement of tokenized transactions, as the city’s monetary regulator prepares to bring regulated stablecoins into trials involving tokenized government debt.

On Monday, the Hong Kong Monetary Authority (HKMA) said that it had advanced its EnsembleTX project to a new stage allowing 24/7 real-value transactions. The HKMA said the update would help banks and their clients move funds globally, manage tokenized investments and handle treasury operations outside conventional settlement hours.

Participating banks initially settled payments through Hong Kong’s existing dollar settlement system, which limited settlement hours. When the pilot began real-value trials in November 2025, the HKMA said it planned to introduce tokenized central bank money to allow settlement around the clock.

The HKMA launched Project Ensemble in March 2024 to explore how banks could settle tokenized transactions using wholesale central bank digital money. A sandbox opened that August, allowing banks to connect their tokenized deposit platforms and test transactions.

Speaking at Hong Kong FinTech Week on Monday, HKMA Chief Executive Eddie Yue said the regulator was introducing tokenized central bank money as a “foundation layer” for 24/7 settlement. He said tokenized bonds, money market funds and bank deposits were already being used.

The HKMA also said recognized dealers had completed secondary-market trial trades in tokenized Exchange Fund Bills, a form of short-term debt issued by the regulator. It plans to expand those trials to include regulated stablecoins.

Separately, HSBC Group CEO Georges Elhedery said at Hong Kong FinTech Week that the bank’s recently launched Hong Kong dollar stablecoin, RedCoin, had reached 50,000 users about 10 days after its debut.

“We have 50,000 users,” Elhedery said, adding that HSBC was targeting “multi-million users” as it expands access to the token.

RedCoin’s rollout brings stablecoins to retail payments, while Ensemble’s settlement work has focused on transactions involving banks and tokenized investments.

Elhedery said HSBC was also offering tokenized deposits to wholesale clients in Hong Kong and about half a dozen other jurisdictions, allowing funds to move around the clock.

Related: Hong Kong prepares banks for quantum threats amid tokenization push

The bank launched RedCoin on Oct. 23 for transfers and merchant payments through its PayMe application. HSBC said more than 3.3 million PayMe customers could activate stablecoin wallets to use the token. It also plans to bring the stablecoin into its mobile banking app and allow subscriptions to tokenized investments, including a Hong Kong dollar money market fund share class.

HSBC was among Hong Kong’s first licensed stablecoin issuers in April alongside Anchorpoint Financial, a venture backed by Animoca Brands and Standard Chartered. The city’s licensing regime, introduced in August 2025, requires issuers to meet standards for backing reserves, redemption and risk management.

#5021. Bitcoin Firm Strive Climbs Up the Treasury Ranks With Another Big Buy

Bitcoin Firm Strive Climbs Up the Treasury Ranks With Another Big Buy

Strive, the Dallas-based company co-founded by Vivek Ramaswamy, now holds 43,124 Bitcoin after buying 124 BTC more last week. That stash is now worth roughly $3.3 billion, and good for about 0.2% of the 21 million coins that will ever exist.

That leaves it fourth among publicly traded companies, 390 coins behind Twenty One Capital (which holds 43,514 BTC) and 876 BTC behind Metaplanet (which holds 44,000 BTC), according to Bitcoin Treasuries data.

At Bitcoin’s price of about $77,000, those gaps are worth roughly $30 million and $67 million, respectively.

Strive is a Bitcoin treasury company—a publicly listed firm that keeps Bitcoin on its balance sheet, so anyone with a brokerage account can get exposure by buying its stock. Michael Saylor’s Strategy popularized the playbook back in 2020, and several other companies followed suit, including Strive.

Its coins cost an average of $87,785 each, or $3.8 billion in total, against a market value of $3.5 billion on Oct. 23, per its prospectus. Bitcoin has slipped since, so the pile is now worth a bit less than Strive paid for it.

The latest purchase covers Oct. 24 through Oct. 30 and was disclosed today in an SEC filing, the report US-listed companies must submit when something material happens. Strive paid about $10 million, an average of $80,526 per Bitcoin including fees, CEO Matt Cole said on X.

Strive acquired 124 $BTC for $10M at an average cost of $80,526 per bitcoin, bringing total holdings to ₿43,124.⁰⁰- Existing cash funded most purchases⁰- SATS ATM used for first time, representing 100% of weekly ATM proceeds⁰- Amplification Ratio: 62.7%⁰⁰$ASST $SATA $SATS pic.twitter.com/rFrS6y4AVc

— Matt Cole (@ColeMacro) November 2, 2026

Most of the money came from existing cash, which fell from $504.4 million to $490.4 million over the week. Strive also raised money for the first time by selling shares of a new security called SATS on the open market, Cole said, and that made up all of the week’s fundraising. It still holds 505,000 shares of Strategy’s preferred stock, known as STRC, worth about $50 million.

SATS is a preferred stock—a type of share that pays a fixed dividend before common shareholders see anything. Strive sold 7 million of them at $90 each in an October offering that raised about $630 million, and they carry a 15% annual dividend on a $100 stated value, paid every business day from Nov. 9.

On Oct. 26, Strive opened an at-the-market program—a way to sell new shares in small batches at the going price—for up to $1 billion more SATS. The prospectus says Strive expects to pay the dividends mainly by raising more capital, including selling more shares. The company has set aside 18 months of dividends in a reserve and reported no debt as of Oct. 23.

Strive’s amplification ratio, the preferred stock and debt it carries measured against the value of its Bitcoin, stood at 62.7%, or about $62.70 of obligations for every $100 of Bitcoin.

The company’s CEO has said ending 2026 as the second-largest public Bitcoin holder is possible. “I wouldn’t say that’s my base case,” Cole said on the One Chair podcast in September, “but I would say it’s in the possible set of cards.”

Saylor’s Strategy holds 849,327 Bitcoin, nearly 20 times Strive’s pile.

Twenty One Capital still holds the same 43,514 Bitcoin it had when co-founder Jack Mallers stepped down as CEO in July. His exit came as a plan by Tether to merge three Bitcoin businesses collapsed. The stock had fallen more than 80% from its highs, and his successor, Raphael Zagury, has said Twenty One should be judged on cash flow and capital discipline.

Metaplanet, a Tokyo-listed company that pivoted from hotels to Bitcoin in 2024, holds 44,000 Bitcoin after overtaking mining firm MARA in April. It has a stated goal of 100,000 by the end of 2026.

At last week’s pace of 124 Bitcoin, Strive would pass Twenty One in four weeks and Metaplanet in eight, assuming neither company buys more.

#5019. Thoughts About Identity Loss for Divorced Women

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 1, 2026 | Reviewed by Jessica Schrader

When you were a married woman, you may have felt safe and secure and things in your life were predictable. The loss of your husband is so much more than just the end of a relationship. It may feel like it strips you of meaning, and many women feel lost. Who am I now?

Sabrina, a woman in one of my groups, remarked: “I keep saying ‘we.’ We saw that movie. We love that restaurant. We had a great vacation in Punta Cana.” She said she’s struggling with the language and language matters. It would sound weird to say: “I had a great vacation in Punta Cana.” It sounds like she went by herself.

Her operating system is still running on “wife.” She went on to say, “While I don’t consider myself a wife anymore, much of my identity and behavior is woven into being that person.” After 27 years of marriage, she doesn’t know who she is without it.

She said she chokes on saying, “my ex.” Ex-husband conveys what society assumes it means. That you grew apart and worked on it but couldn’t solve the problems. Not at all the reality of a woman who has been abandoned. Plus, she used to be the one people came to for support, the one others could rely on. Now she hates appearing like a hero because she’s surviving as a struggling single mom who needs other people’s help. She has an identity, but it’s not the identity she wants.

You’re at a crossroads. You can’t go back; that’s not an option. But it may feel like if you move on, you are accepting all the hurt that was thrown your way. After all, isn’t moving on exactly what your former husband wants you to do? It may feel that moving on would be not honoring the pain. Maybe part of you doesn’t want to give it up. As long as you are suffering, the world can see what he did to you and shake their proverbial heads saying, “Do you believe what a low-life he is?!?”

You are transitioning from an identity that you cherished, being a wife, to one that you may feel is cloaked in shame, being an ex-wife. But thinking that being an ex-wife is shameful or “less than” is a belief, not a fact. What about if you started to shift your thinking so that you could erase that self-limiting belief and start to neutralize it? If roughly 40% of marriages in the U.S. end in divorce, that means that you are part of a large number of women who define themselves as ex-wife. Can you start re-wiring your thinking so that you can begin to get comfortable in this club you never wanted to join?

It seems to me that two factors are taking place simultaneously. First, there are all the losses that you legitimately need to grieve. The loss of the partner, perhaps the home, financial losses, worries about your children, and the loss of the dream of the future. Those are significant enough.

But do you really need to lose your self-image as a person who is valuable and whose life has meaning, too? Wouldn’t it make it a bit easier if you held your head up high and said to yourself, “I may not have been the perfect wife but he wasn’t the perfect husband either. I tried my best and devoted myself to my marriage. I am deciding to not blame myself for the fact that it ended.”

In other words, to shower yourself with self-compassion as you work through this painful period in your life. When you’re soaked in regret and sorrow, Dr. Kristin Neff, the psychologist who works on self-compassion, would recommend you tell yourself these three things:

And then she’d probably recommend that you put your arms around yourself and give yourself a big long hug. You deserve it.

A divorce is the death of a dream. Kristin Neff would remind you that you cannot heal a broken heart by beating it up. Fierce love for yourself and gentle acceptance are the only ways forward, and over time, you will stumble across your new identity that you can learn to cherish, just as you are.

#5018. Trezor lifts password ceiling after Coldcard’s $100M wallet thefts

Trezor cited July’s Coldcard attacks, in which strong passphrases helped protect some users whose wallet recovery information was compromised.

Hardware wallet maker Trezor has increased the maximum length of optional wallet passphrases from 50 to 128 characters.

On Sunday, Trezor said the change is available in its Trezor Suite with updated device firmware. Older firmware still limits passphrases to 50 characters.

According to Trezor, a passphrase acts like an additional password that users set alongside their wallet’s recovery phrase. An attacker who obtains the recovery phrase would still need the correct passphrase to access funds in that wallet.

Trezor cited the Coldcard attacks in July, when a firmware flaw caused some devices to generate recovery phrases with insufficient randomness, allowing attackers to reconstruct them. Trezor said strong, unique passphrases protected some affected users.

The update follows a separate warning involving Trezor’s own devices.

On Friday, the company advised customers who bought wallets from former authorized reseller CryptoBilis since March to move their funds to new wallets.

An Indonesian customer reported a suspected implant inside a Trezor Safe 5 that could capture on-screen information, including recovery phrases, and transmit it over a cellular network. Trezor said Friday it had not examined the device and had received no reports of related losses.
